2.4 	SPATIAL EXTENT OF THE SEARCH
Site-planning projects come about in one of two ways: A client may already own a site and
the future uses of the land have yet to be determined, or the client has predetermined
objectives and a site must be found to accommodate them. Depending on the client’s goals,
potential sites may be widely dispersed geographically or they may all be located within one
large, contiguous parcel. Consequently, site selection involves two possible courses of action:
selecting a site from among two or more noncontiguous sites or selecting a site from within a
larger contiguous parcel—possibly already owned by the client.
Potential sites are either selected or rejected based on each site’s suitability for the
intended uses. This screening process may span a range of spatial scales, involving the
analysis of not only the sites but also the communities in which the sites are located. In
today’s global economy, businesses that are expanding or relocating may evaluate sites in
several countries. Site selection decisions at the national and regional scales consider con-
textual data aggregated for large geographic areas, such as states, counties, and municipal-
ities. Subsequently, additional data are collected on potential sites within each desired area
(Case Study 2-1).
A site selection process may also evaluate sites that are under a single ownership. This
commonly occurs on projects initiated by public institutions that have relatively large land
holdings. Colleges and universities often face this challenge. Campus expansion or rede-
velopment, for example, may involve the construction of new student housing, research or
classroom space, or facilities for recreational and intercollegiate athletics. On a university
campus covering an area of several hundred acres (or hectares), multiple potential building
sites may be available for any given project. Yet, these sites can vary dramatically in their
suitability for the proposed uses. On a hilly site, for example, development constraints for a
proposed dormitory might include the soil’s high susceptibility to erosion or the site’s poor
accessibility for the disabled. On a relatively flat site, in contrast, constraints might include
poor drainage and susceptibility to flooding. These are just some of the many existing
physical factors that can potentially influence the siting, the design, and—ultimately—the
function of either a new building or outdoor facility.
2.5 	THE SITE SELECTION PROCESS
Selecting the most suitable available site for a land development or redevelopment project
has several potential benefits. These include:
Greater convenience for the site’s users
Enhanced aesthetics
Fewer negative environmental impacts
Reduced costs for permitting, construction, operation, and maintenance
Site Selection and Programming 	43

-- 61 of 387 --

The site inventory process documents key biological, physical, and cultural attributes of
the site and its immediate surroundings. Nevertheless, several site and contextual attri-
butes are considered, though usually in less detail, during the site selection process. These
attributes include the endogenous (on-site) as well as exogenous (off-site) factors that
determine the site’s suitability for the intended project.
The site selection process has six discrete steps:
1. Clarify project objectives and site requirements.
2. Choose the site selection criteria.
3. Collect site data and select potential sites.
4. Evaluate each site’s suitability.
5. Rank the alternative sites and select the best.
6. Test project feasibility.
These steps are discussed in more detail over the next several pages and are summarized
diagrammatically in Figure 2-5.
2.5.1 	Step 1: Clarify Project Objectives and Site Requirements
The site selection process is initiated by the client. Project objectives can vary widely among
different clients, which can include public agencies, nongovernment organizations, and
privately owned businesses.
Public sector projects involving site selection include the acquisition or development of
facilities, such as libraries, schools, and parks. Businesses—whether owned by individuals,
partnerships, or corporations—engage in site selection to relocate or to expand into new
markets. Virtually any type of business may engage in site selection, ranging from small retail
and service sector operations to large international corporations.
Real estate development is another activity that regularly involves site selection. “Loca-
tion, location, location”—as the old adage goes—are the three most important factors in real
estate. Location has a very significant effect on property value. For this reason, site selection is
a critical step in the real estate development process.
2.5.2 	Step 2: Choose the Site Selection Criteria
Site suitability is partially a function of site conditions, contextual factors, and expected costs.
The site’s capacity to sustain the proposed uses or activities depends, in part, on the site’s
physical conditions, which include the site area, solar access, and available municipal utilities
and services. Site suitability is also a function of the site’s social, regulatory, and environment
contexts (for example, the scale and architectural character of the surrounding neighbor-
hood). Finally, site suitability depends on the specific costs and benefits of the proposed
project at that site (for example, costs for site acquisition, design and engineering, and
operation and maintenance).
44 	Site Analysis

-- 62 of 387 --

Many social, economic, and environmental factors can influence the success of a land
development or redevelopment project. Choosing an appropriate set of selection criteria will
narrow the search and streamline the process of evaluating and comparing potential sites and,
ultimately, selecting the best available site. This process addresses three crucial issues:
Capacity: Does the site provide adequate physical conditions for the project?
Compatibility: Is the proposed project compatible with the surroundings?
Affordability: Do the project’s benefits outweigh its costs?
Analyzing key site and contextual attributes, or “threshold” criteria, can make the site
selection process more efficient. Frequently, a relatively small number of attributes will have
a disproportionately large influence on the site selection decision. These include essential site

or contextual conditions that, if absent, exclude those sites from further consideration.
Conversely, undesirable site or contextual conditions could, if present, make these sites
unfeasible for the program under consideration.
Site Conditions
Subsurface conditions are important in the site selection process. Shallow depths to bedrock
or groundwater, for example, can reduce a site’s suitability for constructing buildings,
foundations, and underground utilities. Urban sites can present other complications for
development, but these sites may offer excellent redevelopment opportunities. Typically,
urban brownfield sites have access to a full range of public services and utilities. Brownfields
are properties that, because of prior land uses either on or near the site itself, contain haz-
ardous pollutants. Various federal, state, and local brownfield programs can help facilitate the
redevelopment of these sites. In the state of Wisconsin, for example, a “Land Recycling
Law” (Wisconsin Act 453) had two major objectives:
Encourage local governments to take title to environmentally contaminated land and
restore the sites to productive use.
Exempt municipalities from responsibility for remedial action if the property is
acquired through a tax delinquency proceeding or by order of a bankruptcy court.
Public sector brownfield redevelopment programs reduce financial risks and allow
remediation—in certain situations—to conditions that are still contaminated but develop-
able. Instead of requiring complete waste removal from the contaminated site, certain types
and quantities of wastes are allowed to remain underground if steps are taken to prevent
stormwater infiltration into the contaminated soils. An impervious barrier, such as a com-
pacted clay layer, will control leaching and inhibit the movement of chemical contaminants
into the groundwater.
Parcel size or area is another threshold criterion in selecting sites for some purposes. For
example, in selecting sites for public schools in the state of Alaska, the minimum required
land area for a public school site varies with the type of school that will be constructed
(Table 2-2). Unfortunately, this is an example of how site selection criteria can contribute to
urban sprawl. Prospective school sites of 30 acres (12.1 hectares) or more will be typically
available only on the suburban periphery, where very few—if any—students will have the
opportunity to walk or bicycle to school.
TABLE 2-2 	Site area standards for selecting public school sites in the state of Alaska.
School Type 	Minimum Site Area
Elementary School 	10 acres (4.0 hectares) 	1 acre (0.4 hectares) for each 100 students
Middle School 	20 acres (8.1 hectares) 	1 acre (0.4 hectares) for each 100 students
High School 	30 acres (12.1 hectares) 	1 acre (0.4 hectares) for each 100 students
K-12 School 	20 acres (8.1 hectares) 	1 acre (0.4 hectares) for each 100 students
Source: Alaska Department of Education (1997, p. 5).
46 	Site Analysis

-- 64 of 387 --

Other criteria may be important in selecting sites for other uses. For example, the
American Society of Golf Course Architects (2000) suggests that sites selected for golf course
development should have the following:
A visually interesting landscape (for example, rolling hills) that will minimize earth-
moving operations and reduce construction costs
Adequate soil drainage and quality topsoil, which are essential for growing fine turf
Sufficient utility availability (for example, electricity and potable water)
Convenient access to transportation infrastructure, which is necessary to attract golfers
at various skill levels
Contextual Factors
Access to transportation infrastructure remains an important site selection criterion for
most land uses. A site’s access to sufficient utility service is another important factor. These
services include the delivery of potable water, electricity, and natural gas, as well as the
collection and treatment of wastewater. Telecommunication technologies greatly expand
the range of suitable locations for some commercial activities. Access to high-speed
Internet connections is not ubiquitously available and may therefore be an important site
selection criterion.
Visibility to a site from adjacent streets or other off-site locations may be an asset, par-
ticularly if retail or other patron-dependent businesses are being considered for the site.
Visual quality may also influence site selection decisions. Natural and cultural amenities often
enhance property values and contribute to a site’s unique sense of place. Major water bodies,
forests, and mountainous or hilly terrain are amenities that add value to nearby properties,
especially those with unobstructed views of the amenity.
A context-sensitive approach to site selection also considers the compatibility of the
proposed uses with the surrounding area. For example, criteria for selecting prison sites in the
United States include site conditions as well as broader social conditions. These site selection
criteria include (Ammons et al., 1992) the following:
Proximity to the communities from which most inmates come
Areas capable of providing or attracting qualified professional staff of racial and ethnic
origins compatible with the inmate population
Areas with adequate social services, hospitals, schools, universities, and employment
opportunities to support the correctional goals
Contextual considerations also include the site’s visibility from adjacent roads and highways
and the ease with which views to a new prison can be screened (Krasnow, 1998).
Government permitting and approval processes also influence some site selection deci-
sions. The approval process for a redevelopment project, for example, can be delayed or
halted by neighborhood concerns about vehicle traffic, building height, or development
density. Potential project delays can be enough to steer prospective developers away from
municipalities with protracted development review processes.
Site Selection and Programming 	47

-- 65 of 387 --

If all relevant site and contextual factors could be measured quantitatively, the process of
selecting the best available site would be relatively straightforward. Most site selection
decisions, however, involve not only financial but social, environmental, and ethical con-
siderations as well.
2.5.3 	Step 3: Collect Site Data and Select Potential Sites
On some projects, the client may identify potential sites (Figure 2-6). In other cases, the
potential sites must be discovered (Figure 2-7), often through spatial analysis using geo-
graphic information systems (Castle, 1998). Data sources that can be useful in finding
potential sites include the following:
Multiple listing services (MLS) for property that is for sale or rent
Maps of vacant, infill, and redevelopable land prepared by local municipalities during
comprehensive plan updates and neighborhood planning studies
Local governments are generally supportive of development and redevelopment that add
jobs and contribute to local property and sales tax revenues. In addition to providing maps of
Figure 2-6 	Potential sites (east and west) for a new mental health center on an existing health sciences campus in the state of
Illinois. Source: The HOK Planning Group. Used with permission of The HOK Planning Group.
48 	Site Analysis

available infill and redevelopment sites, planning and economic development agencies may
provide other useful data about the sites, including parcel area, available utilities, and
applicable development regulations.
2.5.4 	Step 4: Evaluate Each Site’s Suitability
Once potential sites have been identified, additional data are needed to assess each site’s
suitability for the proposed project. Evaluating site suitability typically involves the analysis of
both quantitative and qualitative data. Some common data sources include:
Aerial photographs
Parcel boundary and tax assessor data
Highway maps
Utility maps
Topographic maps
Soils maps
Ground-level photographs showing views on- and off-site

A property boundary survey is commonly required for the sale of a property and may be
available for sites under consideration. A topographic survey, with detailed data on site
elevations and major tree locations, usually is not acquired until after a site has been selected.
Public investments in schools and parks can have significant impacts on a community’s
fiscal and natural resources, and these investments should be informed by a rigorous site
selection process. The challenge of selecting a school site is reflected in the following quote
from the Alaska Department of Education (1997, p. 2):
The perfect school site can be envisioned as generally level with some topographic
interest, having complete utilities, stable, well-drained soils, excellent road and
pedestrian access, protection from excessive weather patterns, [and] with ample space
for school facilities, [a] playground, and sports fields. The site would be accessible to
present and future populations and be free of any natural or environmental hazards. It
would be removed from undesirable business, industry and traffic hazards but con-
venient to important facilities and recreational/outdoor learning areas. In most
communities, however, the perfect site is elusive and difficult to find.

“Threshold criteria” are critical site and contextual attributes that aid in screening
potential sites. If the threshold criteria are met by one or more sites, the site evaluation
process can proceed to other selection criteria. Conversely, if a site does not satisfy the
threshold criteria, then that site is eliminated from further consideration. Threshold criteria
can be physical, economic, or legal attributes of the site or its surroundings. The flooding
risk, for example, is considered in siting public schools in Alaska (Table 2-3).
The city of San Francisco adds to its public open space system through a systematic land
selection process. Priority is given to significant natural areas that are not otherwise protected
from degradation or development and to open spaces, facilities, and other property in
neighborhoods that are either “high-need areas” (as designated in the Recreation and Open
Space Element of the City’s General Plan) or are experiencing a significant increase in
Site Selection and Programming 	51

-- 69 of 387 --

residential population and have few open spaces or recreational resources (San Francisco
Recreation and Park Department, 2006, p. 3). The process of evaluating sites for potential
open space acquisition in San Francisco employs an extensive set of suitability criteria (see
Sidebar 2-1).

I. MINIMUM CRITERIA
All sites must meet all of the “base” or threshold criteria to be considered for further evaluation. This ensures
that the sites meet at least the minimum standards for site size, maximum slope, absence of unstable soils or
other hazardous or toxic conditions, adequate sunlight, and pedestrian access. Evaluation of the sites that are
then considered for acquisition employs a more detailed set of suitability criteria.

Once a small number of potential sites have been chosen, more detailed studies assess the
fit between each site and the intended program. When sites are being evaluated for multiple
uses, the site selection process becomes more complex because different land uses may
require different conditions. One approach is to prioritize the proposed land uses and
then solve the site selection problem for the most important land use. Sites considered
suitable for the primary land use can then be considered for the next most important
land use, and so on.
2.5.5 	Step 5: Rank the Alternative Sites and Select the Best One
Site suitability ratings are summarized in a matrix as either numerical or graphical values. The
site evaluation matrix lists the potential sites and each site selection criterion (Figure 2-8).
There are no inherent limits on either the number of selection criteria or the number of sites

that can be evaluated. There are practical limits, however, dictated by the time and resources
available, as well as by the risks of selecting a poorly suited site.
When criteria are rated on a quantitative scale, the scores may be combined into a single
site suitability index that is either weighted or unweighted. Simply adding the suitability
scores for all selection criteria is one approach, yielding a total “unweighted” suitability score
for each site as follows:
Site suitability score unweighted 	X 1 	X 2 	X 3 	X n
where X 1, X 2 . . . X n are suitability scores for the selection criteria.
While this approach is attractive for its simplicity, combining the ratings for diverse
selection criteria has drawbacks. Site conditions, cost factors, and contextual factors are
lumped together in one total score, potentially masking important information about the sites’
suitability for the intended uses. Moreover, the unweighted approach gives equal importance
to each selection criterion. Some criteria, however, are more important than others.
Figure 2-8 	Site selection matrix comparing criteria ratings for the two alternative sites. Source: The HOK Planning Group. Used
with permission of The HOK Planning Group.
54 	Site Analysis

-- 72 of 387 --

An alternative approach is to “weight” each criterion according to each attribute’s per-
ceived importance to the site’s suitability for the proposed uses. The algorithm for a
weighted site suitability score is as follows:
Site suitability score weighted 	X 1 W 1 	X 2 W2 	X 3 W 3 	X n W n
where X 1 , X 2 . . . X n are suitability scores for the selection criteria, and
where W 1 , W2 . . . W n are criteria weights.
The weighted approach gives the most important selection criteria greater influence.
Because the scheme for weighting the selection criteria can dramatically influence the eval-
uation results, the weights must be chosen very carefully. On public sector projects especially,
this requires discussion and often negotiation among the various stakeholders. Table 2-4
shows an example of a weighting scheme used to evaluate and select potential school sites.
Another approach is to rate each criterion and condense these ratings into one summary
index that combines the site evaluation scores for social and land use factors, construction cost
factors, and maintenance and operating cost factors (Table 2-5). This approach calculates
separate scores for each grouping of selection criteria. Rather than relying on a single index to
summarize the diverse effects of biophysical and cultural factors on the proposed uses, the site
selection decision is a more nuanced assessment of social, economic, and environmental factors.
Regardless of which approach is taken, the sites must be ranked individually or divided
into ranked groups of sites. In a study of potential sites for the Hood Canal Bridge, for
example, the Washington State Department of Transportation (2005) analyzed 18 sites but
did not weight the suitability criteria or compute a summary score for each site. The sites
were evaluated and placed in one of four ranked categories:

Construction Cost Factors 	3 	4 	5 	3

Grand Totals 	10 	11 	13 	10

2.5.6 	Step 6: Test Project Feasibility
Once the best site has been selected, the project’s feasibility may be studied in more detail
before the site is purchased and the project moves to subsequent phases of the site-planning
process. Whether it is a private sector or public sector project, feasibility studies examine the
proposed project’s costs and benefits. A project’s financial feasibility is determined, in part, by
the physical characteristics of the site and by contextual factors, including the market for
potential customers, competing projects in the area, and regulatory constraints, as well as the
community’s expectations and attitudes concerning land development. Feasibility studies
may include the following:
Market analysis
Assessment of site- and context-specific constraints and opportunities (for example,
risk assessment)
Pro forma financial statements (private sector development) or capital budget/funding
estimates (public sector project)
Design concepts (for example, concept plans and three-dimensional simulations)
On a typical real estate development project, the feasibility study assesses whether or not
the selected site will “pencil out,” or yield the expected return on investment (ROI). The
pro forma statement is the spreadsheet that summarizes the project’s anticipated cash flow,
showing projected expenses, revenues, and profits (Peiser, 1992). This financial statement is
needed to obtain mortgage financing commitments and analyze risks associated with the
proposed development.
The feasibility study also supports other decision making, including (1) how much of the site
can be developed (for example, physical and regulatory constraints limit the developable area,
to some extent, on most sites); (2) what regulatory permits and approvals are needed to develop
the site; and (3) estimates of the time and political obstacles or incentives that can be expected
(Triad Associates, 2001). If the site has not yet been purchased, the client may then decide to
buy it. In some situations, the client may acquire adjacent land parcels to assemble a larger site
with greater development potential. But if the constraints for the proposed project are too
formidable, the client may decide against purchasing the site.
Site Evaluation, Acquisition, and Holding Costs
Site acquisition costs include consultants’ fees for site evaluation and selection. The purpose
of this investigation, or “due diligence,” is to ensure that all potential risks and costs have
56 	Site Analysis

-- 74 of 387 --

been anticipated before acquiring the property. For sites with a history of previous com-
mercial or industrial uses, considerable time and effort may be devoted to site evaluation. An
option to purchase a site is one way that a prospective buyer can reduce the financial risk. If
the client decides not to buy, the financial “exposure” on the project is more limited. After a
site is purchased, land holding costs accrue as the development process continues. These
include property financing costs and real estate taxes.
Site Design and Engineering Costs
As cities grow, land parcels that were “passed over during the first wave of development tend
to be developed later, at higher densities” (Peiser, 1992, p. 48). These sites may have
remained undeveloped for a variety of reasons. A landowner may have simply held the land
for speculative purposes in anticipation of the property’s increasing value. In other cases, sites
are undeveloped because they have significant development constraints.
Difficult sites are more costly to develop for several reasons. Rugged topography,
unstable soils, or shallow bedrock are just a few of the physiographic constraints that
make project design, permitting, and construction more complex and, therefore, more
expensive. Yet, these vacant parcels are often attractive for development because they
are well served by existing public infrastructure and are often located in areas convenient
to cultural amenities and lifestyle conveniences.
Although most sites can be engineered to accommodate the construction of buildings,
roads, and related infrastructure, the development of environmentally sensitive sites can
have negative on-site and off-site consequences. Potential impacts include the destruction
of plant and wildlife habitat and loss of local biodiversity. Life safety risks may also result
from site disturbances that increase the likelihood of landslides or other environmental
hazards.
Permitting and Approval Costs
Not all project costs can be quantified monetarily. Environmental degradation from land
development transfers costs to society rather than to the responsible parties. These negative
impacts are externalities—the result of what economists call “market failures.” A funda-
mental role of local government is to protect public health, safety, and welfare by mini-
mizing these negative externalities. Development impact fees are one way that governments
attempt to link these costs to the responsible parties.
As more has been learned about the built environment’s impacts on ecosystems and
human health, public scrutiny of development proposals has increased. Yet, values and
attitudes toward new development still vary widely among communities, resulting in
differing approaches concerning where, when, and how development is allowed to
proceed.
Objections to development or redevelopment proposals often rest on concerns about the
projects’ off-site impacts. These impacts may affect local environmental quality, the com-
munity’s fiscal well-being, or neighboring residents’ quality of life. Logically, then, the site
selection process must consider the project’s potential off-site impacts. Neighborhood
concerns can prevent a project from being approved or result in substantial revisions to the
project’s scope. NIMBYism (Not In My Backyard) is a substantial challenge to many urban
and suburban redevelopment projects.
Site Selection and Programming 	57

-- 75 of 387 --

Construction and Mitigation Costs
Site preparation and construction costs typically cover excavation and site grading,
stormwater management, site lighting and utilities, buildings, circulation systems, and
landscaping. Site constraints may substantially increase these costs, particularly if devel-
opment is planned for areas that are poorly suited for construction. These constraints range
from poor soil drainage or shallow depths to bedrock to the potentially life-threatening
hazards of flooding and landslides. Ian McHarg’s credo (1969), “design with nature,”
remains an ethically compelling and financially sensible site-planning objective. Designing
with nature reduces development impacts on the web of biophysical systems that func-
tionally link land, water, and biota. Conserving a site’s natural features—such as major trees
and landforms—can also make the remaining developed site a much more desirable place
to live, work, and play.
Mitigating the project’s negative impacts may be required as a condition of project
permitting and approval. Many development projects increase the demands on public
services and facilities. “Exactions” are payments to compensate communities for the
increased costs of providing public services for new development (Altshuler and Gomez-
Ibanez, 1993). Exactions often include payments for off-site infrastructure, such as street
intersection improvements, new traffic signal installations, and utility system extensions.
Exactions may also include impact fees—or dedication of land in lieu of payments—for
public schools and neighborhood parks.
Depending on the municipality in which the project is located, school district impact fees
can amount to several thousand dollars per proposed dwelling unit. For example, a 2005
survey of jurisdictions in King County, Washington State, found that school district impact
fees for a new single-family home ranged from none in the Seattle district to more than
$5000 in parts of the Auburn district (Triad Associates, 2005). Impact fees for public services
such as fire and police protection are also quite common, regardless of the project type.
Chapter 9 includes additional discussion of project implementation issues.
Operation and Maintenance Costs
Maintenance and other project operational costs are also influenced by site conditions.
Building heating and cooling costs, for example, are affected not only by the building’s
design and construction but also by the building’s siting. When continuing life-cycle costs
are considered in site selection decisions, sustainable, or green, approaches to development
may be more cost effective than conventional resource-intensive alternatives.
Environmental hazards that can increase maintenance and operating costs include
flooding, soil erosion, and wildfires. Sites that are subject to natural hazards impose signif-
icant continuing costs. If hazard insurance is available, premiums will be higher than for
comparable property that is not located within a hazardous area.
Other commonly considered factors include corporate tax rates, the available workforce,
and utility capacities and rates. For a heavy-manufacturing facility, for example, proximity to
essential raw materials may be the most important criterion in the site selection decision. This
is particularly true if the raw materials are bulky and expensive to transport. In contrast, for a
firm that manufactures precision instruments, a location near an available supply of skilled
labor—such as tool and die makers—would be more important.
58 	Site Analysis

-- 76 of 387 --

Locational Preferences and Benefits
The value of commercial real estate is strongly influenced by its accessibility. According to
Site Selection magazine’s annual survey of developers, owners, managers, and promoters of
office buildings, proximity to an interstate highway interchange was a key contextual
attribute in the 1990s. The demand for visibility and easy highway access was reflected in
office building site costs (O’Connor, 1996) as follows:
Our survey results show that with increasing distance from the interstate there is a steady
decline in the average price per acre of prepared sites. For sites less than one mile from the
interstate, the average price per acre is $53,000. For sites between one and five miles from
the interstate, the average asking price drops to $45,000 per acre, and for sites between
five and 10 miles from the interstate, the average price dips to $31,000 per acre.
Preferences for residential locations vary widely among different segments of society, or
“markets,” which are largely defined by age, occupation, education level, income, and
family status. For younger couples with children, for example, housing preferences tend to
be influenced by the proximity and quality of primary and secondary schools and outdoor
recreational opportunities.
Development Incentives
Economic development incentives can play a significant role in selecting sites for commercial
and industrial development. According to the International Economic Development Council,
the main goal of economic development is to improve “the economic well-being of a com-
munity through efforts that entail job creation, job retention, tax base enhancements and quality
of life” (www.iedconline.org). The rationale for land development incentives rests on the
economic benefits that may accrue to the state, county, or municipality offering the assistance.
State, county, and local incentives include tax abatements, fee waivers, low-interest loans,
and off-site improvements to public infrastructure (Haresign, 1999). Development subsidies
may also include site preparation costs, assistance with the site plan review and permitting
process, and even site selection services. These subsidies are usually based on the expected
economic benefits from the new businesses. Economic benefits are typically measured by
easily quantified metrics such as the number of jobs created, the expected payroll, or the
revenue generated by selling a publicly owned facility (Moore, 1998).
Economic development programs can create jobs and stimulate capital investment, yet
these programs may precipitate the relocation of businesses from one community to another
(Buss, 2001; Guskind, 1989). Moreover, these business-friendly policies may pay little or no
attention to the environmental or public health implications of the resulting development.
For example, many suburban “office parks” in America are single-use developments
accessible only by private vehicles. These developments tend to have large surface parking
lots and minimal pedestrian infrastructure. Not only do outlying office parks reduce workers’
opportunities for physical activity, these low-density developments also limit access by
potential employees who do not own vehicles. In one case in Minnesota, for example, more
than 20 businesses relocated from an urban site to a distant suburban office park that had no
access to public transportation (Libby et al., 2000).
Site Selection and Programming 	59

-- 77 of 387 --

A site evaluation report is often prepared to summarize the site selection methods and results
for review by the client and other stakeholders. A typical site evaluation report (Sidebar 2-2)
includes both text and graphics (Wike and Bowers, 1995):
2.7 	CONCLUSION
Site selection is the process of identifying a suitable parcel of land for one or more specific
purposes. A site’s location influences the complexity and cost of planning, permitting, and

implementing a land development or redevelopment project. Land use regulations and
cultural, economic, and biophysical attributes all influence—to varying degrees—the site’s
feasibility for the intended project uses.
Site selection involves the collection and analysis of both site and contextual data.
Determining which site and contextual attributes to evaluate depends, in part, on the
program, or intended uses of the site. Project programming considers user needs, the site and
its context, and both successful and unsuccessful design precedents.
Case Study 2–1 	Site Selection and Programming
ARLINGTON C OUNTY B OATHOUSE
SITE PLANNING : EDAW, INC., ALEXANDRIA, VIRGINIA
ARCHITECTURE : RUMMEL , K LEPPER & K AHL , LLP, B ALTIMORE , MARYLAND
BACKGROUND
The U.S. Congress initiated this study to assess the potential for siting a boathouse (rowing facility) within
Arlington County along the Potomac River. The facility would be located along the Virginia side of the river on
public lands managed by the National Park Service. Four sites were considered (Figure 2-9).
PROJECT SCOPE
This study included the development of a minimum and a maximum program for the potential Arlington County
Boathouse. These two programs were used to analyze the feasibility of each study site. The criteria used to
assess each site’s suitability for the boathouse addressed physical conditions, visual/cultural resources, envi-
ronmental impacts, economics, and operational issues.
PROGRAM/PROJECT REQUIREMENTS
Developing the program for the boathouse involved several steps, including:
Reviewing a study prepared by Arlington County’s Water-Based Recreational Facility Task Force in 1995
Interviews with crew coaches at the three Arlington County public high schools
Precedent studies of three area boathouses and one area boat club
Based on this information, the minimum boathouse program included:
Existing boat storage needs and immediate expansion plans of the crew teams at three Arlington County
public high schools
Exercise areas, lockers, showers, and storage space for the three schools

-- 79 of 387 --

Case Study 2–1 	Site Selection and Programming (continued)
Boat-repair area
Minimal office space
Access for trailers, school buses, and emergency vehicles
Gas storage area
Outdoor rigging areas
The maximum boathouse program included all of the above requirements along with additional boat
storage for other high school crew teams in the area, individual rowers, and other community rowing programs
(Figure 2-10).
FACILITY AND SITE ANALYSIS
The analysis of the four sites included assessments of site constraints, conditions of the river for rowing,
transportation access, required infrastructure improvements, and potential visual impacts (Figures 2-11, 2-12,
and 2-13). Schematic site plans were prepared for a minimum program and a maximum program at each site.

Assessing the Site’s
Physiographic Context
In the end, our society will be defined not only by what we create, but by what we refuse
to destroy.
—John Sawhill, former president, The Nature Conservancy
3.1 	INTRODUCTION
Every site is embedded within a landscape, and a key goal in site planning is to understand the
site and its surroundings. Both basic and applied research have contributed to our under-
standing of the physical, biological, and cultural environments. This continually expanding
knowledge base is the foundation for land planning, design, and management theory. The
American Heritage Dictionary of the English Language (3rd ed.) defines theory as follows:
1. A system of assumptions, accepted principles, and rules of procedure devised to
analyze, predict, or otherwise explain the nature or behavior of a specified set of
phenomena.
2. A belief that guides action or assists comprehension or judgment.
The field of medicine clearly reflects the synergistic relationship between theory and
practice. Research on heart function, blood chemistry, and other aspects of human physi-
ology informs the medical practitioners’ health care decisions. The natural and social sciences
similarly provide the theoretical foundation for actions that shape the built environment in
ways that can sustain current and future generations.
71

-- 89 of 387 --

Redeveloping a parcel of land requires information about the site as well as the site’s
surroundings. Many site attributes, such as vegetation type and slope, are unevenly dis-
tributed spatially. Some attributes, such as seasonal temperatures and precipitation, show very
little spatial variation at the site scale. Temperature and precipitation can vary dramatically, of
course, throughout the year. This temporal variation may affect the uses of the site from
season to season. Consequently, it may be appropriate to document the spatial distribution of
a particular attribute at more than one time of the year. Site attributes that vary seasonally
include wildlife distributions, wind direction and speed, sun and shade patterns, and
groundwater levels. Because site planning and design involve land use decisions affecting
people, an understanding of human behavior, attitudes, and preferences is also necessary.
The site inventory is a focused process of collecting and mapping essential contextual
information. It is not a “fishing expedition” or an open-ended investigation of all biophysical
or cultural phenomena that occur on or near the site. The project program, although subject
to revision as more site information becomes available, helps to narrow the scope of this data
collection effort.
3.1.1 	Base Maps
One of the first tasks in a site inventory is to develop a base map. The base map serves as the
template for attribute mapping and analysis, as well as for subsequent site planning and design
drawings (see Chapter 1). If a topographic survey of the site is available, the base map will
typically include project boundaries and other surveyed information (Table 3-1). Topo-
graphic surveys, for site-planning purposes, are usually made by a licensed land surveyor

according to specifications tailored to the program and the site. Oblique and vertical aerial
photos of the site can provide additional contextual information (Figure 3-1).
Rectified aerial photos are particularly good base maps to overlay inventory, analysis, and
conceptual design graphics (Figure 3-2). The size and orientation of the base map depend
largely on the size and shape of the site, the complexity of the program, and the importance
of nearby off-site factors. Ideally, a single size and orientation are used consistently for the
entire set of illustrative materials. Maps and plans are normally oriented so that north is at
the top of the sheet.
The size of the base map is also a function of the map’s scale. A land use planning project
for a relatively large area (for example, several hundred acres or hectares) could conceivably
have a relatively small-scale base map (for example, 1:200 or 1:400). Conversely, a site-
planning project for a residential or commercial building on a much smaller area (for
example, 1 acre or hectare) could have a relatively large-scale base map (for example, 1:20 or
1:40). The base map typically includes the following existing site conditions:

3.2 	PARCEL SIZE AND SHAPE
Land development—and redevelopment—occur over a range
of site scales. A single-use commercial project, for example, can
be designed and implemented on a site of less than 1 acre (0.4
hectare). In contrast, a single-family housing project may be
planned for a site of 20 acres (8.1 hectares) or more.
Parcel size is an intrinsic constraint on a site’s development
potential. If all other factors are equal, larger sites can accommodate more extensive and
more diverse development than smaller sites. On smaller sites, off-site factors are more likely
to influence the site’s design. Therefore, two or more contiguous parcels of land are
sometimes combined to create one larger parcel under a single ownership. Larger sites
generally allow greater flexibility in arranging program elements on the site. Additional site
area also allows for the integration of green space, including natural areas and new plantings
to separate incompatible uses, screen undesirable views off site, and filter and retain
stormwater.
The shape of the site also can reduce development potential and design flexibility. This is
especially true on smaller sites and on narrow linear sites that have a higher edge-to-interior
ratio than properties that are more compact in shape (Figure 3-4). A greater proportion of
edge increases the site’s exposure to the surrounding landscape. If the site is adjacent to a busy
highway, for example, a linear or small site will substantially limit the site planner’s ability to
buffer traffic noises. However, if the site is adjacent to a natural amenity, a parcel with a
relatively high edge-to-interior ratio will benefit from this spatial relationship, particularly if
the amenity is likely to persist well into the near future (Figure 3-5).
In combination, a site’s size and shape can substantially affect its suitability for potential
development. Municipal zoning regulations, for example, typically impose building setbacks
from front, side, and back property boundaries. These restricted areas may occupy a rela-
tively large percentage of a linear site’s total area and render the site unfeasible for devel-
opment from a financial perspective. Context, therefore, is especially important when
evaluating small or linear sites.
3.3 	TOPOGRAPHY
Understanding the site’s topography is essential in making good site-planning and design
decisions. Topographic surveys provide critical information about the site’s biophysical
context. Three key attribute maps can be derived from a topographic survey to graphically
show variations in elevation, slope, and aspect.
3.3.1 	Elevation
Changes in elevation affect both drainage patterns and visibility to and from the site. Ele-
vation data are portrayed on topographic maps as contour lines and spot elevations. For site-
planning purposes, an effective way to visualize topographic relief is to create a choropleth
map of elevation. To make it easy to understand, the map should have relatively few (five to
Edge = 36%
Interior = 64%
Edge = 46%
Interior = 54%
Figure 3-4 	Relationship
between parcel shape and
edge-to-interior ratio for two
parcels of equal area.
76 	Site Analysis

-- 94 of 387 --

nine) elevation classes. The range of existing elevations on and adjacent to a site determines
the range of each elevation class. For example, if the highest elevation on the site is 1327 feet
(404.2 meters) above a local benchmark and the lowest elevation is 832 feet (253.6 meters),
the map must show a range of elevation of at least 495 feet (150.9 meters) (1327 	832
495). To limit the map’s visual complexity, 495 can be divided by six classes for a result of
82.5 feet (25.1 meters). To create elevation classes of equal increments, 82.5 can be rounded
up to 100. To enhance the map’s effectiveness (Figure 3-6), each “layer” is then shaded or
colored—typically with a spectrum ranging from cool colors (for lower elevations) to warm
colors (for higher elevations).
3.3.2 	Slope
Differences in soil parent materials and weathering account for characteristic landforms or
landscape “signatures.” The landforms also reflect the area’s surficial geology. Landforms
result from constructional processes (for example, wind deposition) and destructional pro-
cesses (for example, soil erosion) acting on underlying geologic structures (Bloom, 1978).
A site’s suitability for roads, walkways, buildings, and other structures is, in part, a
function of the existing slopes on the site. In Hong Kong and San Francisco, cities with
relatively warm climates, development frequently occurs on sites with relatively steep slopes.
Figure 3-5 	Coastal and inland water bodies are amenities that can significantly increase the value of adjoining properties.
Waterfront property, however, may also face elevated risks of flooding.
Assessing the Site’s Physiographic Context 	77

-- 95 of 387 --

In contrast, in locations with freezing winter temperatures, vehicle and pedestrian circulation
systems on steep gradients can create safety hazards from icy, sloping surfaces.
Spatial variation in a site’s elevation creates slopes that have both a gradient and an
orientation. Slope gradients can be computed and easily mapped with most geographic
information systems (GIS) and computer-aided design (CAD) software (Figure 3-7). The
range of each mapped slope class depends on the intended uses of the site and on the specific
site and contextual conditions, including soil characteristics, vegetative cover, and regulatory
requirements. For example, to prevent significant environmental and aesthetic impacts,
development regulations may prohibit construction on very steep slopes (for example,
greater than 25 percent). Soil stabilization and erosion controls may be required on moderate
to steep slopes (for example, 8 to 15 percent or 15 to 25 percent). Conversely, sites that are
essentially flat (less than 1 percent slope) may be poorly drained. Each of these slope con-
ditions warrants mapping, because these locations have significant site planning implications.
3.3.3 	Aspect
A slope’s orientation, or aspect, is the compass direction that the slope faces (for example,
north or northeast). Variations in slope and aspect influence the daily and seasonal solar
Figure 3-6 	Choropleth map showing six elevation classes. Each class represents 100 feet (30.5 meters) of elevation change.
Source: The HOK Planning Group. Used with permission of The HOK Planning Group.
78 	Site Analysis

-- 96 of 387 --

radiation received by the site. In the Northern Hemisphere, north-facing 10-degree
slopes will receive far less solar radiation than south-facing slopes of the same gradient
(Figure 3-8). Because the slope faces away from the sun, the solar radiation striking a north-
facing slope hits the surface at a shallow, or acute, angle. Consequently, sunlight strikes
the slope in a more diffuse pattern, delivering to the surface less solar energy per unit area
(Figure 3-9).
Aspect influences microclimate by affecting the amount of solar radiation striking the site.
As with other physical attributes, the importance of a slope’s aspect depends partly on the
proposed uses of the site. At higher northern latitudes, for example, south-facing slopes are
better suited for siting buildings that will incorporate active and/or passive solar heating.
Conversely, a north-facing slope may be better suited for ski trail development in order to
limit the melting of snow in late winter and early spring.
Slope aspect, like slope gradient, can be mapped with commercially available GIS soft-
ware. Typically, aspect is classified using eight categories: north, northeast, east, southeast,
south, southwest, west, and northwest. These are portrayed graphically by either shading or
color. More shaded northern slopes (in the Northern Hemisphere) are typically rendered
with cooler colors or heavier hatching than are the other slopes with greater solar exposure.
Figure 3-7 	Choropleth map of a slope gradient. Source: The HOK Planning Group. Used with permission of The HOK
Planning Group.

3.4 	GEOLOGY, HYDROLOGY, AND SOILS
3.4.1 	Geology
Surficial geology is concerned with the structure, composition, and stability of the materials
beneath and—in some locations—at the earth’s surface. Bedrock geology has a persistent
effect on landforms due to the different rates of weathering that occur on the soil parent
materials. Landform classification is useful in regional assessments, particularly for charac-
terizing attributes that are difficult to quantify, like scenic beauty. Eskers, kames, and
moraines, for example, are distinctive landforms created by prior glaciation. Landforms, in
conjunction with vegetation, define viewsheds and can increase visual quality. Landforms
also influence microclimate, stormwater runoff and infiltration, and the distribution of plant
and animal species.
Soil formation, soil erosion, and soil deposition are natural processes that involve rock
fragmentation and weathering. Weathering occurs unevenly because of variations in the
bedrock’s chemical composition and structure. An important attribute of surficial geology is
the depth-to-bedrock. In some landscapes, bedrock lies many yards or meters below the
ground surface. If excavation for building foundations or for other site structures is planned
for a site with shallow bedrock or glacial eratics (boulders), blasting or other special methods
of removal may be necessary. The cost of excavating a cubic yard or meter of rock is many
times greater than the cost of excavating the same volume of soil. Because these subsurface
conditions significantly increase the costs of building construction, the depth-to-bedrock is
typically investigated.
Geologic maps show the age and distribution of rock layers and other geologic materials.
A volume of rock of a specific type and age range is considered a geologic unit. Colors and
letter symbols identify each geologic unit at or near the land surface. Therefore, a sandstone
of one age might be shown as one color, for example, while a sandstone of a different age
might be shown as a different color. A geologic map usually includes information on
topography and cultural elements, such as roads, to help orient the map user.
A searchable catalog of paper and digital geologic maps is available online through the
United States Geological Survey (see Appendix B). Several map themes in the database are
potentially useful in site planning and design (Table 3-2). These geologic attributes influence
a site’s suitability for excavation and grading, wastewater disposal, groundwater supply, pond
construction, and other common land development objectives (Way, 1978).
A site’s subsurface conditions may be assessed, in greater detail, in conjunction with the
topographic survey. This investigation usually requires the services of a geotechnical engi-
neer. Data on depth-to-bedrock, for example, are collected by drilling bore holes with a
mechanical driller. These samples provide information about the site’s soil and surficial
geology (Joyce, 1982). The spacing and number of boreholes depend on the conditions of
the site and the purposes of the investigation. Sites with great variability in soil and geologic
conditions may require more intensive subsurface investigation. Similarly, when large
buildings will be constructed on a site, the area and depth of the subsurface investigation will
be greater than if the project did not include these structures.
Volcanic activity, earthquakes, and landslides are relatively common events in some parts
of the world. These geologic disturbances are potentially devastating hazards that must be
Assessing the Site’s Physiographic Context 	81

-- 99 of 387 --

considered when planning new development in these regions. The locations of potential
geologic hazards, such as landslides, are often mapped by government agencies involved in
preventing or minimizing natural disasters (Figure 3-10). Geologic investigations may
include section diagrams showing locations that are susceptible to earthquakes and other
hazards.
3.4.2 	Hydrology
Water circulates in the environment through precipitation, overland flow, infiltration,
storage, and evapotranspiration. Groundwater moves by capillary action through the porous
spaces between unconsolidated sand, gravel, and rock, and between the fractures and faults
in the underlying bedrock. The water table, or upper surface of the groundwater resource,
generally mirrors the surface terrain. In landscapes where groundwater is the source of local
or municipal wells, groundwater pumping can substantially increase the depth to the water
table.
Topographic variation creates drainage patterns, which, in turn, influence plant dis-
tributions. The spatial correlation between vegetation associations and site drainage patterns
is particularly strong in arid and semiarid landscapes where water is often the primary limiting
factor on plant growth and distribution. Although the groundwater-vegetation linkage is
more subtle in less arid environments, the continuous or seasonal saturation of soils can
create wetlands. In coastal environments, brackish or saline surface and groundwater result in
the development of salt marshes and other distinct wetland communities.
Without on-site and off-site mitigation, urban development can have significant impacts
on local and regional hydrology. These negative impacts include (United States Environ-
mental Protection Agency, 1993):
Increased volumes and rates of runoff discharges
Reduced time needed for runoff to reach surface waters
Increased frequency and severity of flooding
Reduced streamflow during prolonged periods of dry weather
TABLE 3-2 	Selected map themes of the National Geologic Map Database.
Category 	Theme
Geology 	General (for example, rocks and sediment at or near the earth’s surface); Structure (for example, thickness
of buried rock units); Engineering (for example, engineering properties of soils); Coastal (for example,
areas of coastal zone erosion or sedimentation)
Natural Resources 	Nonmetallic resources (for example, sand and gravel); Water (for example, groundwater aquifers and water
quality); Other (for example, geothermal resources)
Hazards 	Earthquake hazards (for example, faults and earthquake zones); Volcano hazards (for example, eruption
history); Landslides (for example, existing landslides and landslide potential)
Source: United States Geological Survey (2000).

Land development—until relatively recently—involved the construction of buildings and
paved surfaces that are impervious or nearly impervious. These land cover changes sub-
stantially increased the risks of flooding, erosion, and other ecological impacts on properties
“downstream” (Figure 3-11). Surface water pollution associated with stormwater runoff
reduces the aesthetic, recreational, and ecological value of rivers, lakes, and other water
bodies. Consequently, stormwater management has become a highly regulated aspect of the
land development process.
Land development may also negatively impact groundwater quality. When local
groundwater is the source of a community’s potable water, efforts must be made to ensure
that on-site wastewater treatment systems and stormwater runoff do not contaminate local
wells (Figure 3-12). For decades, siting requirements for on-site wastewater treatment sys-
tems used soil conditions as the primary siting criterion. As treatment technologies evolved,
more highly engineered on-site wastewater treatment systems—and associated buildings—
Figure 3-11 	Impervious surfaces in a watershed potentially increase stormwater runoff and reduce soil infiltration and
groundwater recharge. Source: Federal Interagency Stream Restoration Working Group.
84 	Site Analysis

-- 102 of 387 --

have been constructed on formerly unsuitable sites (LaGro, 1996). These sites include
locations with bedrock and/or water tables as shallow as 20 centimeters (8 inches) below the
ground surface.
Stormwater movement, infiltration, storage, and discharge are considered in the site
inventory. The assessment of hydrologic conditions requires analysis of the site’s surface and
subsurface features, including topography, vegetation, surface water distribution, land use,
climate, and soil-forming processes and deposits (Kolm, 1996). Surface drainage patterns
(Figure 3-13) and potential flood hazard zones (Figure 3-14) are typically mapped. These
areas serve critical ecological functions and, therefore, comprise major development
constraints.
Aquifer recharge areas are also important to identify and protect from development. The
primary paths of groundwater flow and the locations of groundwater discharge to the surface
are also important, particularly in more rural locations. Subsurface geologic characterization
may include the analysis of aquifer permeability, thickness, and discontinuities (Kolm, 1996).
Maps of groundwater and local geological conditions are particularly important in land use
Figure 3-12 	To protect the quality of potable water supplies, land use controls often limit development in areas near community

planning for rural areas without access to municipal water service (Figure 3-15). These site
data can help determine potential sources of potable groundwater (Figure 3-16).
3.4.3 	Soils
Climate, landform position, and parent material are key physical factors in soil genesis and
morphology. Other factors include the growth, death, and decomposition of vegetation,
microorganisms, and other biota living above ground or within the soil itself. Soil properties
are also affected by the previous and current land uses (Ferguson, 1999). For example, sites
with a history of previous industrial or commercial uses may be contaminated with a variety
of hazardous substances, and investigation of a site’s subsurface conditions is warranted. If
hazardous wastes are present on the site, the costs of remediation can be substantial.
Depending on the site’s location and the intended land use program, the inventory may
include the following soil attributes:
Acidity/alkalinity (pH)
Permeability
Erosion potential
Figure 3-13 	Map showing watershed boundaries (ridges) and major drainage patterns (valleys). Source: The HOK Planning
Group. Used with permission of The HOK Planning Group.
86 	Site Analysis

-- 104 of 387 --

Depth to seasonally high-water table
Depth-to-bedrock
Subsurface conditions affect not only the complexity of excavation and construction but
also the project design (Figure 3-17). To ensure that the integrity of a new building or
structure is not compromised, foundations in colder climates are designed to a depth below
the lowest frost level. This depth may be 3 or more feet (about 1 meter) below the ground
surface. For large multistory buildings, foundations are designed to considerably greater
depths for structural stability.
Soils vary widely in texture, fertility, permeability, and other attributes that influence
plant growth and development. Soils that are favorable for plant health reduce the impact of
pests and diseases and, therefore, can reduce pesticide use. Soil erosion frequently occurs
when vegetation cover is removed or substantially damaged during site clearing and con-
struction. Topsoil losses from prior agricultural uses or other erosive forces can increase the
costs of reestablishing vegetation on a site after construction.
Bacteria and other microbes in the soil naturally decompose wastes. This essential eco-
system service enables the use of soil-based on-site wastewater treatment systems (OWTS). If
these systems will be installed on the site, the soils must be evaluated for their permeability
and capacity to remove chemical and pathogen contaminants from wastewater effluent. Very

rapidly draining soils, typically those with high sand and gravel content, may be poorly suited
for conventional on-site wastewater treatment (Figure 3-18). Similarly, impervious soils,
such as hardpan, are also constraints that limit on-site treatment without the use of more
highly engineered alternative wastewater treatment systems.
Many landscapes outside of urbanized areas have had soils inventoried and mapped. The
U.S. Department of Agriculture’s Soil Survey maps are spatially coarse (for example,
1:15,840 scale) and do not capture the fine-scale spatial variability that occurs in the depth-
to-bedrock, depth-to-water-table, and other key attributes. However, these soil maps—and
the soil ratings for specific land use types—can be useful in assessing a site’s suitability for the
proposed uses (Figure 3-19).

A site’s expected climatic and microclimatic conditions can influence various site-planning
and design decisions, including the following:
Building orientation and façade design
Siting and design of vehicular and pedestrian circulation systems
Selection and placement of plant materials
Design of the stormwater management infrastructure

Seasonal and monthly climate data are available from national weather services (for

additional information about the weather conditions that can be expected in each season.
Relevant data include the following:
Temperature (maximum, minimum, and day/night temperature variation)
Humidity (high, low, and averages)
Wind (maximum, average velocity, and direction)

period)
Snowfall (monthly total and maximum for any 1 day)
Solar radiation (monthly average)
Potential extreme weather hazards

Over the past decade, evidence of climate change has been mounting. The most recent
report of the International Panel on Climate Change (IPCC, 2007) documents a broad range
of substantial risks to property, life, and livelihoods. Yet, the potential positive and negative
impacts of climate change vary temporally and spatially. For example, the U.S. Department
of Agriculture (USDA) Plant Hardiness Zone map shows the locations where different
average annual minimum temperatures can be expected each year in the United States,
Canada, and Mexico. The map depicts 11 zones, based on average minimal temperature
ranges (for example, -10 to -20 degrees Fahrenheit or -23.4 to -28.8 degrees Celsius for
Zone 4). The plant hardiness map was recently revised, however, to reflect changing climate
conditions (Figure 3-20). This has led to some significant changes in plant hardiness zone
classifications (Table 3-3).
Regional climatic conditions do not account for local variations in microclimate.
Microclimate has a particularly important effect on two aspects of the built environment:
energy consumption for the heating and cooling of buildings, and the comfort of people in
outdoor settings. Both energy consumption and outdoor microclimate are influenced by the
location and design of the buildings and outdoor spaces. Depending on the height of nearby
buildings, trees, or other vertical elements, shadows of varying lengths may be cast across the
site on sunny days.
Shadow length varies daily and seasonally, especially in higher latitudes (Figure 3-21).
The solar exposure of outdoor spaces near buildings varies not only with weather conditions
but also with time of day, day of year, and location of the space in relation to the buildings
and to the sun (Figure 3-22). Slope and aspect also influence surface temperatures through
their effect on the amount of solar radiation striking the site’s surface.
Energy consumption for heating and cooling buildings can be reduced through both
passive and active solar designs. Greater energy efficiency can be achieved passively by
moderating the solar radiation striking and entering a building and by moderating winds and
breezes near the building. In environments where winds are strong and persistent, significant
modifications of building forms may be necessary.
Ambient air temperatures in outdoor areas depend, in part, on whether the space is
exposed to full sunshine. Surface materials and vegetation within the space also affect air
temperatures. Paving materials like brick and stone absorb solar radiation, reradiate the
energy as heat, and thereby raise the air temperature immediately above the paved surfaces.
The heat index is an estimate of “apparent temperature”—the temperature that one feels
because of the interaction between air temperature and relative humidity. Direct sunlight
can raise heat index values by as much as 15 	F (United States National Oceanic and
Atmospheric Agency, 2000a).
Trees, for example, intercept the solar radiation that would otherwise strike pavements,
rooftops, and other surfaces. Inorganic surfaces, especially if they are dark in color, absorb
and subsequently reradiate more heat energy than vegetation. Plant leaves also tend to lower
air temperatures through evapotranspiration. In addition to moderating air temperature and
humidity, plants can improve air quality by removing chemical pollutants and, as a result of
photosynthesis, by adding oxygen to the atmosphere.
Metabolic energy (generated within the body) and radiation (from the sun and from the
earth) are the main sources of energy available to heat a person. A person is thermally
comfortable when there is a balance between the body’s heat losses and heat gains. Energy

from wind, and radiation.

Microclimate can vary greatly over short distances and
over short time spans due to variability in a site’s exposure to
solar radiation and wind (Brown and Gillespie, 1995). In
outdoor rural and suburban environments, solar radiation and
wind are the microclimatic factors that can be most easily
modified by design. Buildings, trees, walls, and other vertical
elements cast shadows, and understanding sun and shade patterns is essential in site planning
and design. Winter winds can be buffered somewhat with windbreaks, and land uses can be
arranged to reduce the negative effects of winds on outdoor activities.
A solar radiation map can be created in a GIS by overlaying and combining three
attribute layers: slope gradient, slope aspect, and vegetation (deciduous and coniferous trees
primarily). The complexity of the solar radiation map depends on the number of classes in
each input layer. If each of these three layers has several classes, the potential number of
combinations would be relatively high. For example, the slope gradient map could have
three classes (0–10 percent, 10–20 percent, and greater than 20 percent); the slope aspect
map could have eight classes (for the eight compass points); and the tree cover map could
have four classes (no tree cover, deciduous trees, coniferous trees, and mixed deciduous/
coniferous trees). If these three layers were combined, the resulting map of all slope, aspect,
and tree cover combinations could have 96 classes (3 classes multiplied by 8 classes mul-
tiplied by 4 classes).
Although useful as an intermediate layer, the solar radiation map would be difficult to
interpret. This map could, however, be reclassified, or recoded, to more clearly convey the
site’s microclimate. One possible map class, for example, would portray all site areas with less
than 10 percent south-facing slopes and no tree cover. Another class would portray all areas
with greater than 10 percent north-facing slopes and coniferous tree cover. In the northern
latitudes, these two areas would receive markedly different levels of solar radiation during the
winter months. Another approach is to aggregate the various slope, aspect, and vegetation
combinations into one of the following four classes (Beer, 1990):
Very warm areas
Warm areas
Cool areas
Very cool areas
Prevailing summer breezes and winter winds can be shown as directional arrows
(Figure 3-23). Because wind is highly variable in both speed and direction, it may be
depicted graphically with “wind rose” diagrams that show the frequency distribution of the
wind direction, velocity, and duration at a specific location.

Assessing a site’s microclimate is challenging, because a variety of sun, shade, and wind
patterns can occur on a site. The position of the sun above the horizon changes seasonally
and daily from sunrise to sunset. Solar access is a particularly important microclimatic factor
in urban settings, where buildings may be either immediately adjacent to the site or on the
site itself. When the development program includes outdoor spaces for seating, dining, and
other activities, shade patterns should be evaluated for several times of the day and year when
people are likely to use the outdoor spaces (such as plazas and terraces). Shade diagrams
might be created, for example, for these four times of the day:

The summer and winter solstices occur on about June 21 and December 21, respectively.
The equinox, when day and night are equal in length, occurs each year on about March 21
and September 22.
3.6 	NATURAL HAZARDS
Natural hazards include atmospheric, hydrologic, geologic, and wildfire events that, because
of their location, severity, and frequency, have the potential to adversely impact humans,
their structures, or their activities (Organization of American States, 1990). Natural hazards
present significant risks to human life and property and, therefore, must be considered in the
site-planning process.
The built environment’s vulnerability to hurricanes, in particular, is substantial, because a
large proportion of the world’s population lives near the oceanic coasts. With growing
awareness of global climate change, the environmental, economic, and human impacts of
natural hazards have become an important public policy issue. The Year 2000 Building
Code marks a substantial shift in public policy regarding construction in areas that are prone

December 21

to natural hazards. In an attempt to reduce continued rebuilding in floodplains and other
hazardous areas, the U.S. federal government has purchased many vulnerable properties. The
U.S. Geological Survey has also created a series of maps that inventory the risks of various
natural hazards in the United States. This is potentially useful contextual information for site-
planning projects in areas that are subject to these natural hazards.
3.7 	CONCLUSION
A site’s physical attributes have a substantial impact on how the site should be developed
(Table 3-4). Assessing these physical attributes is shaped by the project’s program and the
characteristics of the site and the broader regional context. Potential data sources include
aerial photographs, topographic surveys, subsurface investigations, and a variety of publicly
available reference maps. Although not always possible due to time and budgetary con-
straints, visiting a site at different times of the year can enhance understanding of the site’s
character, especially its microclimate.

Case Study 3–1 	New Town Planning
INDIAN T RACE
B ROWARD C OUNTY , FLORIDA
OWNER /DEVELOPER : A RVIDA C ORPORATION , MIAMI , FLORIDA
TRAFFIC ENGINEER /E CONOMIST : W ILBUR SMITH AND A SSOCIATES , MIAMI , FLORIDA
ENGINEER : GEE AND JENSEN , WEST PALM B EACH , F LORIDA
LAND PLANNER: ENVIRONMENTAL PLANNING & DESIGN (EPD), M IAMI LAKES , FLORIDA
OVERVIEW
This New Town project was planned for 10,000 acres (4000 hectares) on the western edge of a large, urbanized,
and rapidly growing metropolitan area along the southeastern coast of Florida. Indian Trace is a planned unit
development (PUD) designed to ultimately accommodate about 100,000 residents. A 2-year research, analysis,
and planning effort culminated in the conceptual master plan that integrated new development within an
extensive green infrastructure network. The process involved many local, state, and federal agencies, citizen
groups, and a team of leading planners, engineers, economists, and ecologists. The projected build-out period
for this new town is 40 years.
SITE ANALYSIS
The 16-square-mile (41-square-kilometer) site is bounded by four major existing or proposed highways. To the
north and west of these highways are protected Everglades Conservation Areas. Long-range projections of
population and land use were provided by the Broward County Area Planning Board and the County Planning
and Zoning Department.
The site’s physiographic context was examined during the site inventory and analysis. Data sources included a
detailed topographic survey and aerial photographs, supplemented by field investigations of soil, water, veg-
etation, and wildlife. More than 30 “eco-determinant” maps were created to define the site’s ecological
context and long-range constraints and possibilities. These maps addressed climate (Figure 3-25), hydrology
(Figure 3-26), and other biophysical attributes. No endangered species were found, but vegetation on this site
included an abundance of invasive exotic species (i.e., melaleuca, Brazilian pepper, Australian pine). Relatively
shallow groundwater, low-site elevations in relation to mean sea level (MSL), and abundant seasonal rainfall
were important land use constraints.
Cultural factors influencing the project’s conceptual design included the locations of public facilities and
infrastructure. Regional transportation systems, schools, public safety, and medical facilities were considered,
along with other cultural attributes. For each of these elements, existing conditions, as well as community and
regional plans for the future, were assessed.

Assessing the Site’s Biological Context
The more clearly we can focus attention on the wonders and realities of the universe, the
less we shall have destruction.
—Rachel Carson (1962, p. xix)
4.1 	INTRODUCTION
About 82 percent of America’s population now lives in urban areas. The U.S. population
doubled in the past six decades, growing from 151 million in 1950 to about 304 million
people in 2010. During this period, millions of acres of forests, grasslands, and wetlands in
America were converted to urban, suburban, and exurban development. This unprece-
dented expansion of the built environment—with relatively little attention paid to its
sustainability—eradicated countless habitats and fragmented natural corridors linking terrestrial
and aquatic habitats. Because of habitat loss and fragmentation, ecosystems today are more
isolated from each other—and less buffered from both human and nonhuman disturbances.
In recent years, according to the U.S. Geological Survey, about 10,000 miles (16,000
kilometers) of paved roads and 1 million new homes were built annually. These impervious
surfaces—and many of the largest metropolitan areas in the United States—are located
in watersheds supplying the nation’s major rivers and estuaries. Because of this proximity,
aquatic ecosystems—and, in many communities, municipal water supplies—are at signif-
icant risk.
Ecosystems have both biotic and abiotic components, and the ecological structure and
function of landscapes are influenced by the complex interplay of biological, physical, and
cultural systems (Naveh and Lieberman, 1984; Turner et al., 2001). Landscape ecology is a
transdisciplinary science concerned with landscape structure, function, and change (Forman
112

-- 130 of 387 --

and Godron, 1986). Both basic and applied research in landscape ecology can help to inform
more context-sensitive land use planning at regional, community, and site scales (Dramstad
et al., 1996).
We now understand, for example, that when new roads are built in formerly forested
landscapes, they can be barriers to the lateral movement of ground-dwelling animals, iso-
lating endemic species, and ultimately dooming those species to extinction at the local level
and possibly at broader spatial scales (Figure 4-1). Conversely, roads constructed through
previously undisturbed forests, or other fragmented natural areas, can serve as linear conduits
for pathogens and invasive exotic species (Forman, 2003). The trans-Amazonian highway is
a prominent example of how road development fragments habitats and opens up previously
inaccessible areas to further deforestation and other land cover change (Vieira et al., 2008).
4.2 	NATURE’S INFRASTRUCTURE
Nature’s infrastructure is the air, water, soil, plants, animals, and microbes that—together, in
ecosystems—provide many of the “goods and services” we need to survive on this planet.
Trees, for example, remove carbon dioxide and add oxygen to the air we breathe through
photosynthesis. Soils—which are teeming with microorganisms—help detoxify pollutants in
stormwater runoff. But there are limits to the amount of pollution that the environment,
in any given location, can absorb without reducing ecosystem services and impairing human
health.
Many conventionally designed built environments result in off-site environmental
impacts, such as contaminant-laden stormwater runoff from parking lots and other imper-
vious surfaces. Climate change heightens the risks of this “business as usual”—and resource-
consumptive—development paradigm. Many conventional land use practices not only
imperil environmental quality, but also community livability.
Combined storm and sanitary sewers—an artifact of early-twentieth-century practices in
urban sanitation—exist in nearly 800 cities in the United States, mostly in the northeastern
Great Lakes and northwestern regions (United States Environmental Protection Agency,
2004). Many of the nation’s largest cities—including Washington, D.C.—spill tons of
sewage into the nation’s waterways during heavy rainstorms and snowmelts. These com-
bined sewer overflows (CSOs) adversely impact aquatic ecosystems and the regional
economies that are dependent on these natural resources. In the Chesapeake Bay—the
Interior Habitat 	Edge Habitat
