nation’s largest estuary—crab and oyster fisheries have declined because of polluted runoff
from the built environment and from adjoining agricultural areas. The Bay’s ecological
degradation has diminished the regional seafood industry that relies on its fisheries.
America’s shopping malls, commercial strips, and office complexes typically have too few
trees, too little green space, and vast expanses of impervious pavement that shed rather than
conserve rainwater (Figure 4-2). Studies by the nonprofit Center for Watershed Protection
show that streams, lakes, and other aquatic ecosystems are degraded when just 10 percent of
the contributing watershed’s surface is developed impervious area. Poorly designed urban

and suburban development is the culprit—where buildings, parking lots, streets, and
highways treat rainwater as a waste product rather than as a natural resource.
Fortunately, streets and parking lots can be retrofitted (Figure 4-3). Low-impact design can
reduce “effective” imperviousness and substantially decrease the built environment’s hydro-
logic and ecological impacts. “Green streets” have pervious pavement—and are bordered by
shade trees and bio-infiltration swales, or rain gardens, which filter storm runoff and allow
rainwater to percolate into the ground (Case Study 4-1). Highways can be greened, too, by
planting medians and rights-of-way with native species of trees, grasses, and wildflowers. With
nearly 47,000 miles (75,639 kilometers) of interstate highways, and many more miles of state
and county highways, eliminating mowing on thousands of acres would cut carbon emissions
and help reestablish habitat connectivity—creating ecological “bridges” for species displaced
by the road building, nearby development, and, potentially, a changing climate.
Green roofs—a good example can be found on Chicago’s City Hall—employ an insu-
lating layer of plants and soil that reduces heating and cooling costs and harvests rainwater
that would otherwise flow into nearby sewers. They also can provide usable outdoor space
above the noise and fumes of congested streets. Green roofs help cool cities in summer, too.
Chicago offers grants, and New York City now offers property tax credits to spur green roof
retrofits. These incentive programs are models for the rest of the country.
Greener, more natural approaches to managing riparian zones—protected, ideally, with
conservation easements—can also reduce the quantity and improve the quality of storm
runoff entering surface waters. The value of ecosystem services to human well-being and the
implications of different land use patterns and environmental management approaches are
becoming better understood. Biologically based ecosystem services include food and fiber
production, waste decomposition and assimilation, as well as the provision of recreational
and spiritual experiences in nature (Daily et al., 1999; Reid et al., 2005).
Human systems are integrally connected with natural systems (Folke et al., 2002).
Investing strategically in nature’s infrastructure could be transformative—advancing
Figure 4-3 	A new tree island created within an existing shopping center parking lot.
Assessing the Site’s Biological Context 	115

-- 133 of 387 --

environmental protection while making our communities healthier and more resilient to
climate change (LaGro, 2009).
4.2.1 	Ecological Communities
An ecological community is an aggregation of interacting species living together in the same
place. Complex interactions among climate, soils, topography, disturbances, and the
organisms themselves influence the composition and spatial distribution of ecological
communities (Sadava et al., 2006). These communities are often named for the dominant
plant species, which may be dominant because of either its physical size or its abundance.
Two examples of North American communities are the beech-maple-hemlock and the oak
savanna communities (Carpenter et al., 1975).
Landscape pattern—that is, the spatial arrangement of habitat patches and corridors, along
with the intervening land uses and land covers—strongly influences biodiversity and other
indicators of ecosystem health (Turner, 2005). Ecotones—the spatial boundaries of eco-
logical communities—are especially important areas biologically. Animals may find shelter in
one ecotone but seek food in another habitat.
4.2.2 	Habitats and Niches
William E. Odum (1959), in Fundamentals of Ecology, writes:
The habitat of an organism is the place where it lives or the place where one would go
to find it. The ecological niche, however, is the position or status of an organism
within its community and ecosystem resulting from the organism’s structural adap-
tations, physiological responses, and specific behaviors (inherited and/or learned). The
ecological niche of an organism depends not only on where it lives but also on what it
does. By analogy, it may be said that the habitat is the organism’s “address,” and the
niche is its “profession,” biologically speaking.
The expanding built environment has dramatically altered the structure and ecological
function of landscapes worldwide. Habitat fragmentation, for example, is a global problem
that can be mitigated through the coordinated reintroduction of green infrastructure. Just by
planting trees and gradually increasing a community’s tree canopy, the negative impacts of
habitat loss and impervious surfaces can be mitigated. Planting trees is a stormwater best
management practice (BMP).
Land use changes often impact habitat structure (for example, the pattern, size, and
connectivity of habitat patches and corridors). Landscapes are a kind of dynamic mosaic,
consisting of patches (for example, woodlot) and corridors (for example, stream), and
sometimes a larger, more extensive matrix (for example, cropland). Natural environmental
corridors allow the movement of organisms between habitats on daily and seasonal time
scales. These corridors also enable species migration over longer temporal and spatial scales.
Gaps in environmental corridors, therefore, can be targets for restoration (Hilty et al., 2006).
Habitat connectivity is an important function of regional open space systems, particularly
in the context of climate change, which is altering the geographic range of countless plant
116 	Site Analysis

-- 134 of 387 --

and animal species. Riparian areas and wetlands serve important hydrologic functions, in
addition to their ecological benefits, which include biodiversity (Wilson, 1988). These
natural areas also provide recreational opportunities, aesthetic amenities, and outdoor “living
laboratories” for natural science education (deGroot, 1992).
Simply leaving natural areas untouched may not be enough to ensure their ecological
health. Many forest bird species, for example, require habitats in large forest patches. The
“interior” forest habitats are buffered from predators and from microclimatic fluctuations
that are more common within forest edge habitats. Many animal species also need more than
one habitat type for their different life-cycle stages, such as reproduction and migration.
4.2.3 	Invasive Species
For millennia, plant and animal species have been adapting to habitats altered by human
activities. Centuries of global trade, migration, and settlement have introduced thousands of
nonnative species to new environments. These exotic—and sometimes invasive—species
include megaflora, like the eucalyptus and melaleuca trees in the United States. Other
common invasive plants include vines (kudzu) and shrubs (Brazilian pepper).
An estimated 50,000 species of exotic plants, animals, and microbes have become
established in the United States in both managed and natural ecosystems (Myers, 1979;
Pimentel et al., 2000). Nearly half of the species listed as threatened or endangered under the
federal Endangered Species Act are at risk primarily because of predation by, or competition
for food and space with, invasive nonnative species (Wilcove et al., 1998). Exotic species also
have substantial economic impacts. Annual expenditures for the control of nonindigenous
species are estimated to exceed $6 billion per year in the United States alone (Pimentel
et al., 2000).
Exotic species are particularly successful colonizers when the new habitats present min-
imal competitive controls that limit their growth and reproduction. Invasive species may be
limited in their native habitats, however, by grazing (in the case of plants) or by predation (in
the case of animals). Competition for the same ecological “niche” helps hold in check the
populations of competing species.
The melaleuca tree, now one of the most invasive tree species in the Florida Everglades,
was intentionally seeded there in the mid-twentieth century (Davis and Ogden, 1994).
Without understanding the potential impacts of the plant on the Everglades ecology,
melaleuca seeds were dispersed in an effort to dry the wetlands and create land suitable for
agriculture and urban development. Similarly, the eucalyptus tree was imported into Cali-
fornia from Australia and planted as a new source of timber. In the benign California climate,
and with no significant natural competitors, the eucalyptus species has successfully colonized
many of the state’s native oak forests and savannas (Santos, 1997).
On sites with significant native plant and animal communities, consideration should be
given to mapping the distributions of both native and invasive exotic species. This infor-
mation can be used in developing plans to eradicate invasive exotic species and potentially
restore degraded native communities. Detailed information on the distribution of native and
exotic species must be collected by visiting the site (Figure 4-4). In some contexts, the
restoration of wetlands, prairies, riparian corridors, and/or woodlands may be feasible. Soils,
hydrology, and vegetation maps are helpful in selecting the most suitable locations for exotic
Assessing the Site’s Biological Context 	117

-- 135 of 387 --

species eradication and native species restoration. A chor-
opleth map can show the locations of significant vegetation
over a range of densities (Figure 4-5). In areas where plant
communities have evolved under a fire regime, the fre-
quency of wildfires—a natural hazard for any nearby
development—may also be mapped (Figure 4-6).
Global land cover satellite digital data are available at 30-
meter (98.4 feet) spatial resolution through the U.S. Geo-
logical Survey’s Land Cover Institute (http://www.landcover.
usgs.gov/). In the United States, the National Land Cover
Dataset (NLCD) is a 31-volume compact disc (CD) set
containing 21 categories of land cover, percent tree canopy,
and percent urban imperviousness derived from the imagery.
Large-scale aerial photographs support even more detailed
land cover mapping. Color infrared photographs are partic-
ularly useful in assessing vegetation health.
4.3 	WETLANDS
Since the beginning of European settlement in the early
1600s, the area that is now the coterminous United States
has lost—primarily through agricultural and urban devel-
opment—more than half of the area’s original wetland
acreage (Dahl, 1990). The federal agencies involved in
wetland regulation define wetlands on the basis of hydrol-
ogy, vegetation, and soils. Most states have also developed
regulatory definitions of wetlands, but these definitions tend
to emphasize the presence of certain vegetation rather than
the area’s soils and hydrology. Wetlands classified on the
basis of plant and soil conditions generally fall into one of
the following three categories (Tiner, 1997; United States
Fish and Wildlife Service, 2000):
Areas with hydrophytes and hydric soils (marshes, swamps, and bogs)
Areas without soils but with hydrophytes (aquatic beds and seaweed-covered rocky
shores)
Areas without soil and without hydrophytes (gravel beaches and tidal flats) that are
periodically flooded
The National Wetlands Inventory (NWI), administered by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife
Service, includes information about the extent, characteristics, and status of wetlands in the
United States. The NWI includes an online digital wetlands database for the entire United
States (http://wetlandsfws.er.usgs.gov/NWI/). These maps, although relatively coarse in
spatial scale, provide useful contextual data. The Fish and Wildlife Service’s wetland

classification scheme has five general classes and several subclasses as follows (Cowardin
et al., 1979):
Marine (open ocean and its associated coastline)
Estuarine (tidal waters of coastal rivers and embayments, salty tidal marshes, man-
grove swamps, and tidal flats)
Riverine (rivers and streams)
Lacustrine (lakes, reservoirs, and large ponds)
Palustrine (marshes, wet meadows, fens, playas, potholes, bogs, swamps, and small,
shallow ponds)
A large majority of the wetlands in the United States are in the palustrine system; most of
the remaining wetlands are estuarine. Wetlands perform myriad functions that directly
benefit society. Coastal wetlands, for example, are nursery grounds for shellfish and other
commercial sport fish (Figure 4-7). Other species, such as migratory birds, depend on these
habitats for food and cover. Wetlands also serve as natural retention areas for stormwater
runoff and are often locations of groundwater recharge. Ephemeral wetlands have soils that
are saturated for a relatively brief period each year or, in more arid landscapes, even less
frequently. Although in the dry season these wetlands appear to have very little ecological
value, during wetter periods they are critical habitats for many migratory bird species.
Wetland “banking” is allowed in some jurisdictions for development mitigation purposes.
This controversial practice attempts to create new wetlands in anticipation that existing
Figure 4-7 	Salt marsh—a grassy coastal wetland rich in marine life. Amelia Island, Florida.
120 	Site Analysis

-- 138 of 387 --

natural wetlands will be destroyed through land development. Unfortunately, this practice is
emblematic of a “brute force” approach to land development (Josephson, 2002). Con-
structed wetlands typically develop far less biodiversity than indigenous wetlands. Another
problem with this practice involves the hydrologic context of both the destroyed and created
wetlands. The loss or degradation of natural wetlands can alter local hydrologic regimes,
increase the flooding risk, and diminish groundwater “base” flow into nearby streams and
lakes. Field assessments can locate and assess the status of on-site wetlands (Figure 4-8).
4.4 	WILDLIFE
The populations of most wildlife species are spatially discontinuous. That is, the entire
species consists of spatially dispersed subpopulations called “metapopulations” (McCullough,
1996). Local-scale extinctions of metapopulations are an inherent property of ecosystem
dynamics (MacArthur and Wilson, 1967). This natural process involves temporary local
extinctions, followed by migration and colonization by members of other metapopulations.
It is essential, however, that the area in which a local extinction occurs remains a viable
habitat for that specie’s future colonization and repopulation (Fahrig and Merriam, 1994). If
the habitat is destroyed or degraded, or made inaccessible by intervening barriers, then the
geographic range of the species is permanently reduced.
Wooded patches and stream corridors within urban, suburban, and rural landscapes are
home to colonies of birds and other animal species assemblages. Conserving wildlife habitats
Figure 4-8 	Wetland map prepared in planning a land development project in the state of
Washington. Source: R. W. Thorpe and Associates.
Assessing the Site’s Biological Context 	121

-- 139 of 387 --

within the built environment has benefits for metacommunities—local communities of
multiple interacting species (Leibold et al., 2004). Many bird and bat species, for example,
are prolific consumers of insects that are pests to food crops and people. Birds also have
significant aesthetic value for avid bird watchers and casual observers alike. The maps
showing spatial distributions of known endangered and threatened species can provide useful
contextual information (Figure 4-9). These maps typically show the geographic range where
these species’ habitats and populations are likely to be present. The most effective method for
protecting species—whether or not they are endangered and threatened—is to protect their
natural habitats (Figure 4-10). Consequently, when proposed land development projects
threaten an endangered species, site distribution data for these species are typically acquired
through field studies by qualified biologists. The intent of the federal U.S. Endangered
Species Act (ESA) is to prevent local as well as regional species extinctions.
4.5 	PLANTS
Many communities recognize that trees are valuable natural resources that enhance quality of
life, sense of place, and property values. Municipal street tree inventories commonly record
tree size, species, and location. Increasingly common are municipal tree preservation ordi-
nances that seek to protect trees throughout the development process (Arendt, 1999). In
communities where orchards and/or vineyards contribute to a distinctive sense of place,
development regulations can protect these horticultural features (see Case Study 4-1).
Plants are critically important elements in the built environment’s green infrastructure.
Trees can serve multiple design functions that directly benefit people and the environment.
For example, trees help clean the air and create habitat for insects and birds. Trees also can
serve as wind breaks, provide shade (Figure 4-11), and reduce the costs—and environmental
impacts—of heating and cooling nearby buildings. By moderating the microclimates near
homes and other buildings, trees reduce the energy demand for air conditioning. According
to the Department of Energy, buildings account for about 40 percent of the energy con-
sumed and the carbon dioxide emitted in the United States.
Trees also add significant aesthetic value by screening undesirable views or spatially
defining ‘‘outdoor rooms’’ (Figure 4-12). Trees also benefit public health by tempering the
urban heat island effect and decreasing physical and emotional stress (Martineau, 2011). As
significant amenities, trees can increase the value of real estate by as much as 15 percent
(National Association of Home Builders, 1991). The International Society of Arboriculture
identifies four factors that affect the economic value of trees (Perry, 1999):
Size (mature trees, for example, have greater value)
Species (hardy, well-adapted species are worth the most)
Condition (healthy roots, trunks, branches, and leaves add value)
Location (a prominent specimen tree is of greater value)
Consequently, a healthy tree standing alone is usually worth more—from an economic
perspective—than one that is growing in a group.

Context-sensitive site planning seeks to integrate into the site plan, where feasible, sig-
nificant existing trees and green spaces. In assessing a site’s biological attributes, data on tree
species, size, and condition are typically recorded. A key question, if there are significant
trees on the site, is: can these natural assets be incorporated within the development? Existing
on-site trees require protection during the construction of buildings, utilities, circulation
systems, and other site elements. Construction damage can kill trees outright or lead to their

major site factors that influence plant health should be assessed to inform site-planning
decisions, including plant species selection (Table 4-1).
Site surveys, completed by licensed surveyors as part of the site-planning process, typically
include the locations of trees larger than a specified size (for example, dbh 	4 inches/10.16
centimeters). Tree size is measured by the trunk’s diameter at breast height (dbh). Global
positioning systems (GPS) provide another way to map individual specimen trees and, on
large sites, the boundaries of plant communities. With a handheld GPS receiver, a botanist or
other expert who can identify the local flora walks the site and digitally records the plant
inventory.
4.6 	CONCLUSION
Growing concern for sustainability has led to increasing demands for accountability—and
documentation of the potential social, economic, and environmental impacts of land
development policies and projects. Landscape ecology, a transdisciplinary science, provides a
strong conceptual framework for assessing sites and making land planning, restoration, and
management decisions (Forman and Godron, 1986; Naveh and Lieberman, 1984). Pro-
tecting native plant and animal habitats is not only good for the environment, it also helps
maintain the site’s unique sense of place. Public health experts are also gaining a greater
awareness of the linkages between human contact with nature and human health (Louv,
2011; Millenium Ecosystem Assessment, 2005).
Assessing a site’s biological attributes is an important step in understanding the site, and in
protecting the ecological integrity of the site both during and after the site plan’s imple-
mentation. Which attributes to map and evaluate depends, as with physical and cultural
attributes, on the site’s context as well as the proposed uses of the site.

c h a p t e r 	5
Assessing the Site’s Land
Use, Infrastructure, and
Regulatory Context
The concept of public welfare is broad and inclusive . . . the values it represents are
spiritual as well as physical, aesthetic as well as monetary. It is within the power of the
legislature to determine that the community should be beautiful as well as healthy,
spacious, [and] clean.
—William O. Douglas, U.S. Supreme Court Chief Justice,
Berman v. Parker (1954)
5.1 	INTRODUCTION
Development that fits the site is responsive to site context. Cultural features—whether
historical, aesthetic, legal, or economic—create opportunities and constraints for the design
of sustainable and livable places. An assessment of cultural factors not only describes the site,
but also analyzes its suitability for the site’s intended uses.
5.2 	LAND USE TYPE AND INTENSITY
Adaptively reusing developed, but underutilized, land is a key principle of Smart Growth
and sustainable green design (see Chapter 1). But prior land use on a site can affect devel-
opment suitability by increasing site preparation costs for the demolition and removal of
135

-- 153 of 387 --

buildings and underground structures, and potentially the remediation of the site’s soils. If a
site was previously used for industrial or commercial purposes, this could indicate that
chemicals and other toxic wastes remain on the site, either above or below ground.
Heavy metals and other toxic compounds pose serious human health risks, particularly to
children. Yet, prior to the 1960s, industrial sites in the United States were often literally used
as dumping grounds for manufacturing wastes. In other cases, poorly maintained storage
tanks led to unintended leaks of chemicals into the soil and groundwater. In the 1970s, the
discovery of toxic chemicals oozing from the ground within a housing development in Love
Canal, a neighborhood of Niagara Falls, New York, was a landmark event in the devel-
opment of hazardous waste policy in the United States (Beck, 1979). Federal legislation was
subsequently passed to address the nation’s toxic waste problem.
The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) was charged, in 1980, with admin-
istering the newly created Superfund Program (www.epa.gov/earthday/history.htm).
Although meant to clean up contaminated brownfield sites, this program had unintended
consequences for the nation’s cities. A property’s owners could be liable for cleanup costs
even if they were not responsible for—or even aware of—the original pollution. In addition,
Superfund regulations held these property owners liable for the remediation costs of
restoring the sites to precontamination conditions.
The enormous costs of even small toxic waste cleanups had a chilling effect on the
redevelopment of urban commercial and industrial sites. The legal and financial risks of
purchasing and attempting to develop brownfield sites effectively encouraged urban sprawl
by redirecting new development to greenfield sites on the suburban periphery. Especially in
older cities, vacant industrial sites inhibited economic investment in the surrounding
neighborhoods, thereby, worsening the pollution’s economic and social impacts. Finally,
during the 1990s, a major shift in public policy began to reduce financial risks—and create
new incentives—for the redevelopment of contaminated brownfield sites (National Gov-
ernors Association, 2000).
In the United States, the redevelopment of a brownfield site typically requires an ASTM
Phase One Environmental Site Assessment (ESA), requiring the collaboration of legal,
environmental, and engineering professionals with expertise in regulatory compliance and
project permitting (American Society of Civil Engineers, 1996). ASTM International, for-
merly the American Society for Testing and Materials, develops consensus standards for the
construction industry. An ESA summarizes the site’s ownership and land use history, in
addition to its current soil and groundwater conditions. Buildings and other structures on
the site are also evaluated and mapped. The cost of an ESA in the late 1990s ranged from
$60,000 to $200,000, depending on the size of the parcel, the complexity of the existing site
structures, and the types of contaminants found on the site (Talarico, 1998). Permanent deed
restrictions also may be required to ensure that the contaminated areas remain sealed from
infiltrating stormwater.
In contrast to industrial and commercial uses, prior agricultural uses on a site can create
other environmental challenges. In the early twentieth century, farming in many parts of the
United States was practiced with little concern for soil conservation and management.
Consequently, many previously farmed lands have lost topsoil from extensive soil erosion.
More recently farmed land also may be laden with nutrients and other chemicals from
136 	Site Analysis

-- 154 of 387 --

fertilizers and pesticides. Either of these conditions increases the site development costs
associated with establishing and managing new plantings.
In assessing a site’s land use context, consideration must also be given to the land use types
and intensities on adjoining properties. Intensity, or density, can be measured in several ways.
Three of the most common density metrics are building height or number of stories, floor-
area ratio or FAR (Figure 5-1), and dwelling units (DU) per area (for example, acre
or hectare).
5.3 	PROPERTY OWNERSHIP AND VALUE
Information on land ownership or “tenure” is available in publicly accessible databases (for
example, the county Register of Deeds). These records may include a certified survey map of
the parcel boundaries, a history of land ownership, and any deed restrictions or covenants
associated with the property. Online digital maps and associated “metadata” are increasingly
available commercially and through government agencies.
In the United States, for example, one company sells digital topographic data models that
are viewable as either two- or three-dimensional images (www.delorme.com). This com-
pany also offers digital maps of streets, trails, unique natural features, and historic sites. Other
digital data, such as vertical and oblique color infrared aerial photographs, may be available
for downloading through government sources (for example, the federal Upper Midwest
Environmental Sciences Center, www.umesc.usgs.gov). Banks and other lending institu-
tions routinely require property appraisals before financing real estate purchases. Munici-
palities also evaluate real estate for property tax assessments (Figure 5-2). Real estate appraisal
attempts to predict the probable selling price of a property by modeling, as closely as possible,
the influence of site and contextual factors on buyers’ and sellers’ decisions. In general,
appraisal theory estimates economic behavior in three different ways: the cost approach, the
FAR = 1	FAR = 1 	FAR = 1
FAR = 0.25 	FAR = 2	FAR = 1
Figure 5-1 	Floor-area ratio (FAR) increases as the amount of building floor space increases in relation to the site area.
Assessing the Site’s Land Use, Infrastructure, and Regulatory Context 	137

-- 155 of 387 --

sales comparison approach, and the income approach (Castle, 1998). Each of these appraisal
methods estimates property value based on the “highest and best use” of the site. In other
words, the site’s location and other attributes determine, at least in part, the purposes for
which the property should be used.
Property appraisals consider the size and existing uses of the site as well as the site
improvements, such as buildings and landscaping. Appraisals also consider the monetary
impacts of the site’s location, such as the market value of nearby recently sold properties.
Attractive and well-maintained adjoining properties, and scenic views to off-site built or
natural amenities, can substantially increase a property’s appraised value. But an adjoining
street with heavy vehicular traffic can suppress a residential property’s value. Conversely,
Figure 5-2 	Tax parcel map showing that the assessed value of individual parcels, per unit area, can vary widely.
138 	Site Analysis

-- 156 of 387 --

vehicular traffic can actually increase the value of commercial properties with shops, res-
taurants, and other customer-dependent uses.
Highest and best use reflects the property owner’s objectives, however, not the com-
munity’s. Property uses with high financial returns but significant environmental impacts will
be valued more favorably, for example, in appraisals that ignore these externalities. More-
over, environmental amenities such as parks and other green spaces are not evenly distrib-
uted spatially, and sites vary widely in proximity to those amenities. Scenic views of lakes,
rivers, or other environmental features may contribute significantly to a site’s desirability for
residential uses and for some commercial uses, such as restaurants, where patrons value the
ambience of a beautiful natural setting (Figure 5-3).
Google Earth (www.google.com/earth/index.html) and Google Maps (http://maps
.google.com/) are readily available sources of aerial photographs and, in metropolitan areas,
street-level photographs. The high-resolution photographs are an excellent resource for
visually assessing land use patterns and development character in urban areas. The street-level
photos enable relatively detailed assessments of on- and off-site amenities.
The positive effect of amenities on property value is reflected in the price disparity among
land parcels in coastal areas. Beachfront parcels, for example, sell at a premium compared to
comparable lots that are not immediately adjacent to the amenity. But property value is also
affected by the location’s potential uses. If the beachfront sites were zoned and developed for
some industrial purpose, the scenic waterfront views would play little or no role in the value
of the products manufactured there.
Property values are affected by land use regulations in other ways as well. The purchase of
development rights (PDR), for example, coupled with conservation easements, can prevent
Figure 5-3 	Scenic views from a
site are amenities that can increase
property values and influence the
type and intensity of on-site
development.
Assessing the Site’s Land Use, Infrastructure, and Regulatory Context 	139

-- 157 of 387 --

development for either an unlimited or a defined period of time. The transfer of devel-
opment rights (TDR) is another legal strategy for redirecting potential development from
conservation areas to other, more suitable locations.
5.4 	LAND USE REGULATION
The legal context for site planning and design varies spatially across local, state or regional,
and—especially—national jurisdictions. Land use laws and development standards also vary
over time. The approach taken in this book is to focus on common regulatory objectives (such
as endangered species protection, stormwater management, and walkability) and to provide
some examples of major laws, policies, and permitting procedures from the United States. One
limitation of this approach, of course, is that the legal context in the United States is different
from the legal context in Asia, Canada, or Europe. Nevertheless, land use regulations in other
nations address many of the same themes—namely, the protection of the environment and
public health, safety, and welfare.
Property ownership entails both rights and responsibilities. The “bundle” of development
rights accompanying a property depends upon the parcel’s location as well as its biophysical
and cultural attributes. In the United States, regulations that exceed the basic functions of
protecting public health, safety, and welfare have been challenged in the courts—and upheld
by the U.S. Supreme Court—as property “takings” (Bosselman and Callies, 1972). When
land use regulations have significantly reduced the property’s monetary value, the courts
have generally ordered the regulating unit of government to provide “just compensation” to
the affected landowner.
5.4.1 	Federal Regulations
Although land use control is largely a local government responsibility in the United States,
cumulative land use changes often have regional and even national implications. Conse-
quently, federal and state laws and policies have been enacted to protect environmental
quality and preserve historic and other cultural resources. The National Environmental
Policy Act (1969) was the first in a series of major environmental laws with broad influences
on land use in the United States.
Federal regulations establish standards or administrative rules that are implemented at the
state and local levels. The National Coastal Zone Management (CZM) Program (http://
coastalmanagement.noaa.gov), for example, is a voluntary partnership between the federal
government and U.S. coastal states and territories. Authorized by the Coastal Zone Man-
agement Act, the purpose of the program is to (United States National Oceanic and
Atmospheric Agency, 2000b):
Preserve, protect, develop, and—where possible—restore and enhance the resources
of the nation’s coastal zone for this and succeeding generations
Encourage and assist the states to achieve wise use of land and water resources of the
coastal zone, giving full consideration to ecological, cultural, historic, and aesthetic
values as well as the needs for compatible economic development
140 	Site Analysis

-- 158 of 387 --

Encourage the preparation of special area management plans to protect significant
natural resources, allow reasonable coastal-dependent economic growth, protect life
and property in hazardous areas, and improve predictability in governmental decision
making
Encourage the participation, cooperation, and coordination of the public, federal, state,
local, interstate and regional agencies, and governments affecting the coastal zone
The Coastal Nonpoint Pollution Control Program is one component of coastal man-
agement in the United States. This program requires states and territories with approved
coastal zone management programs to develop and implement methods for coastal nonpoint
pollution control. Techniques to limit the pollution of coastal waters have been developed
by the EPA for five source categories of nonpoint pollution: agricultural runoff, urban
runoff, forestry runoff, marinas, and hydro-modification. In addition, several other federal
laws and policies are designed to protect cultural resources or enhance the quality of life. The
Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA), for example, also has important site development
implications, which will be discussed further in Chapter 9.
5.4.2 	State Regulations
The power to regulate land use in the United States is granted to local governments
through state-level enabling legislation. Local land use regulations are a subset of the
community’s “police powers” to protect public health, safety, and welfare (Garvin,
2002). As the human population continues to increase and the built environment grows
to cover an increasingly large portion of the United States, state-level growth man-
agement programs have added another layer of development controls and, in some cases,
development incentives (Gale, 1992, pp. 425–439). Several states, including Maryland and
New York, are attempting to direct new infrastructure investment to support higher-
density development in areas that are already served by municipal infrastructure. These
Smart Growth programs can reduce inefficient sprawl and facilitate the redevelopment of
existing urban districts and corridors.
5.4.3 	Local Plans and Regulations
Governments are divided into three branches, and each branch has specific functions. In
the United States, the legislative branch in local governments consists of a city council that
writes and enacts local laws (ordinances or codes). Although these elected officials are the
final authorities for regulating land development within the city’s jurisdiction, they typically
are advised by a planning commission of local citizens appointed by the mayor, the head
of the executive branch (Owen, 1994). Many cities have a planning department, also in
the executive branch, with a staff that prepares and administers the planning ordinances
and policies.
Local governments influence the location, timing, and character of new development
with comprehensive plans, zoning codes, subdivision ordinances, and related public policies
(Garvin, 2002; Nivola, 1999).
Assessing the Site’s Land Use, Infrastructure, and Regulatory Context 	141

-- 159 of 387 --

Comprehensive Plans
Comprehensive plans are community-level plans that articulate a vision for how a com-
munity intends to grow and develop, physically and economically, typically over a 20- to
30-year period (Kelly and Becker, 2000). For example, Wisconsin’s state comprehensive
planning legislation (Wisconsin Act 9, adopted in 1999) requires that all local governments
have a comprehensive plan if they engage in programs or actions that affect land use (Ohm,
1999). The act also requires that comprehensive plans address a range of policy issues to
guide future development and redevelopment.
Comprehensive plans address several broad themes that are relevant to sustainability,
including housing, transportation, utilities, natural and cultural resources, and economic
development. Each theme can include specific goals for protecting cultural and natural
resources and promoting sustainable and affordable development. Examples of these plan-
ning goals, which are consistent with context-sensitive and sustainable site planning, include
the following (City of Madison, 2006, p. 3):
Protect Cultural and Natural Resources
Protect natural areas, including wetlands, wildlife habitats, lakes, woodlands, open
spaces, and groundwater resources.
Preserve cultural, historic, and archaeological sites.
Protect economically productive areas, including farmland and forests.
Promote the redevelopment of lands with existing infrastructure and public services
and the maintenance and rehabilitation of existing residential, commercial, and
industrial structures.
Promote Sustainable and Affordable Development
Encourage land uses, densities, and regulations that promote efficient development
patterns and relatively low municipal, state governmental, and utility costs.
Build a community identity by revitalizing main streets and enforcing design
standards.
Provide an adequate supply of affordable housing for individuals of all income levels
throughout each community.
Provide an integrated, efficient, and economical transportation system that affords
mobility, convenience, and safety and meets the needs of all citizens, including transit-
dependent and disabled citizens.
Zoning Codes
Comprehensive plans are typically implemented through zoning codes and supplementary
land use regulations. A zoning code divides a community into smaller planning districts or
zones (Figure 5-4). Although some development standards or requirements apply
throughout a community, additional rules may apply to the individual zones within the
142 	Site Analysis

-- 160 of 387 --

municipality’s jurisdiction. Zoning regulations address three sets of issues within each zone,
which affect potential site development (Meck et al., 2006, pp. 2-1, 2-2):
1. Permitted use of land and buildings.
2. Intensity of the use. For residential uses, intensity is expressed as dwelling unit
density—minimum lot sizes for single-family residential districts and number of
dwelling units per acre/hectare for multifamily residential districts. For nonresidential
districts, intensity may be expressed as floor-area ratio (FAR: total area of interior
building space divided by area of the site) or building height (number of stories allowed).
3. Height, bulk, and other dimensional standards. These standards define, in three
dimensions, the portion of the lot that can be occupied by buildings. They may limit
the percentage of the site covered by a new building, the building’s FAR, and/or the
building’s height and bulk. Other standards influence building siting by specifying
minimum lot sizes and required building setbacks from the front, side, or rear
property lines. Zoning standards may also govern, for example, the design of parking
lots, lighting, and signs.
The maximum allowable building envelope, as prescribed by local zoning codes, can be
illustrated graphically with three-dimensional computer modeling software (Figure 5-5),
such as Google Sketchup (http://sketchup.google.com/).
Since the early twentieth century, zoning codes have been a tool for regulating land uses
and implementing community plans at the local level. Typically, a city’s zoning map may
have hundreds of zones or districts in which certain uses are permitted, other uses are allowed
under certain circumstances, and all other uses are prohibited. Zoning districts are typically
designated with a letter (indicating land use type) and a number (indicating land use
intensity). An “R-3” district, for example, allows residential development up to a medium
density (for example, multifamily attached dwellings).
Each district defined by a zoning code has certain allowable or “by right” uses, such as
residential, commercial, or industrial. A district may also allow “conditional uses” with
approval from local decision-making bodies such as the planning commission and the city
council. Zoning codes typically allow other uses, not specified for a particular zoning district,
through a zoning variance procedure.
Zoning codes allow variances from a district’s code requirements when the decision-
making bodies deem them to be in the best interest of the broader community (Case Study
5-1). Planned unit development (PUD) or planned development districts (PDD) may be
permitted, for example, to allow the consolidation of multiple parcels into a single master-
planned project, often for mixed-use development. The PUD or PDD provides greater
flexibility in meeting the density and land use requirements for the site as a whole.
Zoning codes in the United States have been the target of justified criticism (Kunstler,
1993, 1998). Conventional or “Euclidean” zoning has contributed to suburban sprawl by
separating residential uses from nonresidential uses and by imposing building setback
requirements, density limits, and parking requirements that also make high-quality, efficient
public transit impractical.
Assessing the Site’s Land Use, Infrastructure, and Regulatory Context 	143

-- 161 of 387 --

Zoning code revisions are becoming more common in North America as communities
rewrite these regulations to encourage Smart Growth. To mitigate decades of sprawl-inducing
public policy, some municipalities are focusing more on the “character” or form of new
development rather than on the intended uses of that development (Figure 5-6). Unified
form-based zoning, design guidelines, and design review are tools that these communities are
using to enhance the sustainability and livability of their built environments.
Figure 5-4 	Zoning map
showing the districts described
in the city’s zoning code.

Zoning Overlay Districts
Special “overlay” districts may impose additional development standards or allow greater
development flexibility. In designated historic districts, for example, design options are
restricted to promote the preservation and restoration of buildings with historical signifi-
cance. For more information on standards and guidelines for protecting historic places, see
Canada’s Historic Places (www.historicplaces.ca/) or the U.S. National Trust for Historic
Preservation website (www.nationaltrust.org/).
Other special overlay districts, including transportation corridors and central business
districts, may require new development to conform to context-specific design guidelines.
These special districts are often intended to maintain (or create) a unique sense of place.
“Incentive zoning” may be used in these targeted areas to improve the design and man-
agement of the public realm. Incentives may be granted, for example, to encourage
development that incorporates public open spaces like vest-pocket parks and plazas, or other
public amenities such as atriums and second-story skywalks (Garvin, 1996).
Subdivision Ordinances
Land development often involves the subdivision of one land parcel into two or more
parcels. This is especially common when land is developed for single-family housing where
each house sits on an individual parcel or lot. Land subdivision is a legal process requiring
subdivision plat approval (Figure 5-7). The subdivision plat records the legal boundaries of all
parcels, easements, streets, and street rights-of-way (Owen, 1994). The process generally
begins with a preapplication meeting where the developer or designer and planning staff

Subdivision ordinances regulate site development by
establishing requirements for the shape and size of lot
and street blocks, street and sidewalk dimensions,
parking lot configurations, and building setbacks from
property boundaries. These ordinances may also limit
the number and location of street access points or “curb
cuts” to a property from adjoining public streets; impose
requirements for the installation of street lights, street
trees, and bus stops; and require easements and other
dedicated space for stormwater management (Meck
et al., 2006).
Unified Development Codes
Zoning codes and subdivision ordinances have contrib-
uted to urban sprawl in the United States by separating
residential uses from commercial, recreational, and civic
uses and by imposing design standards for minimum lot
sizes and street dimensions (Figure 5-8) that effectively mandate inefficient, low-density
development (Ben-Joseph and Szold, 2005; Duany et al., 2001).
Unified development codes attempt to eliminate the arbitrary distinctions between the
regulation of subdivisions and other forms of development (Meck et al., 2006). These
standards apply to all development proposals, whether or not they require land parcel
subdivision. In the American Planning Association’s “Model Smart Land Development
Regulations” (Meck et al., 2006, pp. 2–3), the best unified development codes
blend the use and intensity review process (typically a part of the zoning process) with
design review (traditionally part of subdivision review). The integrated review process
allows citizens and neighbors of the proposed development to get a clearer picture of
what is being proposed on the site.
Unified development codes also provide greater predictability and fewer regulatory con-
tradictions for developers and their consultants.
“Form-based” codes can create higher-density, yet more livable, neighborhoods. These
codes recognize the important influence of the built environment’s design on community
health, safety, and welfare. Higher-density development, if well designed and accompanied
by meaningful open space, plantings, and other landscape amenities, creates housing and
lifestyle options that appeal to large segments of an increasingly diverse population.
Depending on the context, higher-density development may even entail single-family

detached housing on small lots. The code revisions needed to allow these more sustainable
forms of development include (Wolcott, 2004, p. 3):
Minimizing right-of-way and pavement width requirements (reduces impervious
surfaces and stormwater runoff impacts)
Reducing building setback restrictions (allows housing on smaller sites)
Allowing required on-site open spaces to include wetlands, woodlands, and other
ecologically significant natural areas (allows higher densities on the site’s “net”
developable area)
Encouraging rain gardens to help manage stormwater runoff from driveways, parking
lots, and rooftops (reduces off-site runoff and the need for large detention ponds)
Allowing smaller dwelling units—about 1000 square feet (93 square meters)—with
single-car garages (recognizes a diverse market segment that does not want or need
large houses and lawns)
Building codes are concerned with the structural integrity of buildings subjected to
various stresses or “loads” from occupants, seismic activity, wind and snow, and the
buildings’ own weight (Owen, 1994). Building codes also address other health and safety
issues, including fire safety, plumbing, electrical power, sanitation, and energy efficiency.
Published national design and construction standards, such as the International Building
Code (IBC), are often adopted by municipalities and supplemented with local standards. In

the United States, the Americans With Disabilities Act (ADA) helps to make public
buildings—and buildings open to the public—accessible to people with impaired mobility.
The ADA is a federal civil rights law (1992) that prohibits the exclusion of people with
disabilities from everyday activities (for example, shopping, attending movies, eating in res-
taurants). ADA requirements affect private businesses of all sizes and nonprofit organizations.
Tax credits and deductions offset many of the costs of providing access to people with dis-
abilities. The ADA has less stringent requirements for existing facilities built before 1993.
Design Guidelines
Neighborhood and building design guidelines are another type of land use regulation that
can influence a site’s development options. Municipalities may use design guidelines and a
design review process to enhance the visual quality of business districts, major community
entrances and corridors, and other public areas (Table 5-1). Design guidelines may specify
desired building massing and facade articulation in downtown redevelopment districts, for
example, or the preferred paving materials and furnishings along the district’s streetscapes.
Design guidelines also may be created by large institutions to ensure that new devel-
opment is appropriate for its physical and social contexts. This is especially important on
university campuses, where new building and site development is influenced over time by
different designers. Universities are increasingly sensitive to physical design because the
aesthetics of their facilities are an important factor in recruiting and retaining students, staff,
and faculty (Kenney et al., 2005).
Building design guidelines can be an extension of the project programming process
(Zeisel and Maxwell, 1993, p. 168):
In order to translate a facilities program into a physical environment for a workplace,
designers need more than numbers and diagrams. They need planning and design
principles that respond to the organization’s style—its culture.

Visual quality is an important concern of design guidelines, but it is not the only objective.
The standardization of campus lighting and benches, for example, can reduce long-term
maintenance and replacement costs.
Design guidelines—or any other standards or expectations that could limit or influence
site and building design—must be assessed in the site inventory process. Public agencies as
well as private institutions with jurisdiction over the property under consideration may
impose a variety of development restrictions. And depending on the site’s legal context,
development proposals may require special permits and other procedures (for example,
variances, design review, public hearings, and environmental tests). The land use regulatory
issues that should be investigated for each site, in addition to the property’s zoning classi-
fication, include easements, covenants, and other deed restrictions.
Covenants
New Urbanist neighborhoods are able to develop consistently high design quality through
the adoption and enforcement of neighborhood design covenants. Typically, a neighbor-
hood architectural review board—comprised of the developer, perhaps, along with staff and
residents with design expertise—reviews plans for new houses or commercial buildings
within the development. The design standards, typically addressing building size, placement,
style, and surface treatments, as well as landscaping, can add substantial costs for design and
construction. These standards tend to increase property values and, correspondingly, reduce
affordability.
Easements
Easements are legal restrictions that reduce a parcel’s development potential (Table 5-2). For
example, a site easement may enable access to an adjoining property that is accessible only by
crossing the subject property. Easements allowing emergency vehicle access or utility
maintenance access are also common. Conservation easements, typically held by nonprofit
organizations, are created to preserve open space and protect ecosystem integrity. Scenic
easements can also limit the scope and character of land development. These development
restrictions must be mapped as part of the site assessment.
TABLE 5-2 	Common types and purposes of easements.
Type 	Purpose
Access Easements 	Allow physical access to, or across, a site from adjacent properties
Utility Easements 	Provide physical access to install, replace, and maintain utility system infrastructure, such as power lines or
underground piping
Conservation
Easements
Restrict development potential; often used to protect hiking trails and other recreational areas, and to
maintain ecosystem functions such as groundwater recharge
Scenic Easements 	Protect vistas and viewsheds by preventing development that blocks or degrades those views
Solar Easements 	Protect solar access to an adjacent property

5.5 	INFRASTRUCTURE
The built environment is a complex mosaic of private and public buildings, green spaces, and
transportation and utility infrastructure. The location and condition of existing transporta-
tion and utility infrastructure influence the siting and orientation of new buildings as well as
other key site-planning decisions, including the location of entrances to the site.
5.5.1 	Transportation
In both site planning and architectural design, especially in urban contexts, established
pedestrian and vehicle circulation patterns must be carefully analyzed and mapped. Different
circulation systems (pedestrians, bicycles, vehicles) and volumes can be portrayed graphically
by varying the color and width of the arrows (Figure 5-9). The average daily traffic volumes
on adjacent streets and highways may be available online from the local municipality’s public
works or traffic engineering department. Existing conflicts between vehicles and pedestrians
also should be mapped. These walkability challenges often can be mitigated through good
site planning and design.
“Location efficiency” considers the site’s accessibility to parks, shops, and other nearby
community and neighborhood destinations. When the costs of housing and transportation
are combined, many houses in outlying suburbs are less affordable than those in inner
suburbs (Center for Neighborhood Technology, 2012). In the United States, a site’s location
efficiency can be evaluated with two online GIS assessment tools: Walkscore (www.
walkscore.com) and “H T Affordability Index” (http://htaindex.cnt.org/).
The locations of existing streets, driveways, transit stops, parking lots, and other infra-
structure inform context-sensitive design. Site planners usually have leeway in determining
how and where pedestrians enter a site, for example, and existing pedestrian, bicycle, and
vehicular circulation patterns should influence these decisions (Figure 5-10). Failing to
anticipate the “desire lines” between existing and/or proposed building entrances and
other destinations may create new pedestrian–vehicle conflicts. Walking distances from
specific destinations can be shown graphically as a dashed circle, typically representing a 5- to
10-minute walk (Figure 5-11).
5.5.2 	Utilities
Many land uses require continuing inputs of resources, such as potable water and electricity.
With increasing attention to resource conservation and sustainability, these inputs may be
generated on-site through cisterns, solar panels, wind turbines, and other small-scale tech-
nologies. In more conventional approaches to development, these resources are delivered to
the site through the utility infrastructure. These utilities may include gas, electricity, tele-
phone, television, and the Internet. Many land uses also generate continuing outputs, such as
sanitary waste that is either treated on-site or collected at an off-site treatment facility.
Typically, major utility lines are located within streets’ rights-of-way or within other
Assessing the Site’s Land Use, Infrastructure, and Regulatory Context 	151

of these grey infrastructure systems may be unavailable. Understanding where the off-site

5.6 	CONCLUSION
Urban and suburban redevelopment is accelerating in many communities and metropolitan
areas around the world. These existing built environments are inherently more complex—
and potentially more challenging—for site planners. Because project programs are also
becoming more complex or “thick” with uses, these challenging contexts increase the
importance of site analysis in supporting evidence-based design that reshapes the structure
and function of the built environment.

Case Study 5–1 	Urban Infill
N ORTHWESTERN MEMORIAL H OSPITAL
CHICAGO , ILLINOIS
OWNER /DEVELOPER : N ORTHWESTERN UNIVERSITY
S ITE PLANNING : T HE HOK P LANNING GROUP , ST . LOUIS , MISSOURI
ARCHITECTURE : ELLERBE B ECKET , MINNEAPOLIS , MINNESOTA
OVERVIEW
Northwestern Memorial Hospital, in cooperation with Northwestern University and its medical school,
commissioned the joint venture team of Ellerbe Becket/The HOK Planning Group to design a new inpatient
pavilion and ambulatory care facility in downtown Chicago. Accommodating the dramatic growth in outpatient
156 	Site Analysis

-- 174 of 387 --

Case Study 5–1 	Urban Infill (continued)
care while providing more cost-effective hospital operations, the new medical complex consolidates services
previously housed in aging facilities located throughout a six-block area. Construction of the new hospital took
place without disrupting patient care at the existing medical facilities on the site.
SITE ANALYSIS
The site for the medical center is in the heart of downtown Chicago, one of the largest cities in the United
States. The planning process for this project included identifying and comparing three potential sites (Figure
5-14). Five different development options for these three sites were also compared (Figure 5-15). Several criteria
were evaluated in making the final site selection decision, including each site’s effects on the medical center’s
cost, image, and growth potential. Convenient access and parking for patients, employees, and visitors were
among the other important site selection criteria.
The site inventory and analysis addressed a variety of physical, social, and legal issues. These studies also
spanned a range in scale. For example, studies of vehicle circulation addressed arrival and departure patterns
within a radius of several miles (Figure 5-16). Other studies of vehicle circulation and access focused on potential
arrival and entry sequences within a city block or two of the medical center site (Figure 5-17). Factors considered
in this analysis included pedestrian circulation patterns, microclimate, visual character of the surrounding
architecture, and site land use regulations.
Figure 5-14 	Map showing potential hospital sites. Source: The HOK Planning Group. Used with permission of The HOK
Planning Group.

-- 175 of 387 --

Case Study 5–1 	Urban Infill (continued)
The planning process for the new medical center involved extensive negotiations with the building’s future
occupants, municipal planning staff, and other community stakeholders. For example, the project’s program-
ming phase involved verifying the current program (for existing facilities), as well as reprogramming specific
components of the existing facilities. One of the spaces designated for reprogramming was the clinical labo-
ratory. The design team chose to develop an on-site core lab, with the majority of the laboratory space located
off-site. Community stakeholders also played a significant role in identifying issues that ultimately influenced
the design of the medical center (Figure 5-18).

DESIGN FEATURES
The new medical complex includes a 526-bed, 1-million-square-foot (92,903-square-meter) hospital, and a
1-million-square-foot (92,903-square-meter) ambulatory care facility. The ambulatory care facility includes
offices and clinical space for the 400 physicians in the Northwestern Medical Faculty Foundation group practice
and 200 additional staff physicians. Special features of the complex include 96 intensive-care-unit beds, all new
diagnostic and treatment facilities, integration of inpatient and outpatient services, and parking facilities for
2100 cars in a multistory parking structure.

1. What are some examples, in your community, of how land use policies may have

Case Study 5–1 	Urban Infill (continued)
The design of the medical center building reflects, in many ways, sensitivity to context (Figure 5-19). For
example, the key guiding principles for locating and designing the medical center included the following:
Reducing bulk by “stepping down” the building toward Lake Michigan
Breaking up the “superblock” by creating separate towers
Creating ground-level east/west pedestrian “malls”
Creating a “gateway” to the medical center
These design decisions were made to achieve specific functional and/or aesthetic objectives, which include
reinforcing the urban street-wall and continuing the Chicago tradition of designing buildings with distinctive
tops.
A project of this complexity requires creativity, negotiation, and responsiveness to the concerns of commu-
nity stakeholders. To achieve greater flexibility in the way the project was designed, the consultants gained a
zoning amendment to reclassify the site as a planned development area (Figures 5-20 and 5-21). The conditions
for rezoning included no net increase in density and a shift in building mass away from Lake Michigan. Other
requirements included improvements to the local pedestrian and vehicle circulation systems (Figure 5-22).

c h a p t e r 	6
Assessing the Site’s Cultural
and Historic Contexts
Cultural resources are those tangible and intangible aspects of cultural systems, both past
and present, that are valued by or representative of a given culture, or that contain
information about a culture.
—United States National Park Service
6.1 	INTRODUCTION
Development that fits the site is responsive to the site’s cultural and historical context. By
incorporating in new development the design styles and construction materials indigenous to
the region, site planners and architects can strengthen a community’s sense of place.
6.2 	BUILDING AND NEIGHBORHOOD CHARACTER
6.2.1 	Morphology
Understanding community character hinges on identifying its defining attributes. Kevin
Lynch (1960), in The Image of the City, developed a conceptual framework to assess urban
structure, or legibility, and to explain how people form cognitive maps, or mental images, of
the built environment. Cognitive maps, Lynch theorized, aid orientation and movement,
or way-finding, in built environments with a strong sense of place. Lynch’s five elements of
urban form, with examples of each, are the following:
167

-- 185 of 387 --

Edges (for example, shorelines, streets, and major changes in land use)
Paths (for example, streets and major walkways or bike paths)
Districts (for example, neighborhoods and major institutions)
Nodes (for example, entrances, plazas, and major walkway intersections)
Landmarks (for example, unique buildings, bridges, and natural features)
This conceptual framework is especially useful in assessing the character of the built envi-
ronment in an area considered for redevelopment.
Figure-ground mapping is another technique for assessing the texture or “grain” of the
built environment. This involves mapping just two elements: the building footprints (figure)
and the spaces (ground) between the buildings (Figure 6-1). The resulting map of solids and
voids reveals the texture of the built environment, which can vary widely across a com-
munity, spatially, from block to block, and across time, from decade to decade. Suburban
buildings, for example, are typically freestanding objects in space, in contrast to urban
buildings, which are often connected and effective shapers of outdoor space (Frederick,
2007). Figure-ground diagrams are particularly useful for informing the design and siting
of new buildings (for example, determining the building’s size, shape, and placement on
the site).
Evaluating the built environment’s existing character can help site planners design con-
text-sensitive new development. If the design quality near the site is good, then this context
may be worth emulating. In some locations, however, the existing built environment is
Figure 6-1 	Figure-ground
diagram showing building
footprints superimposed on a
vertical aerial photograph. This
graphic technique enables a
visual assessment of urban
texture or grain—an important
contextual attribute. Source:
The HOK Planning Group. Used
with permission of The HOK
Planning Group.
168 	Site Analysis

-- 186 of 387 --

poorly designed. An assessment of building character, for example, considers at least the
following attributes (Pregliasco, 1988, p. 15):
Façade height, width, and proportion
Proportions of window and door openings
Horizontal and vertical rhythms
Material colors, shapes, and textures
Roof form
Signs and sidewalk coverings
Setback from street
6.2.2 	Typologies
Evaluating a site’s cultural context can also benefit from the analysis of typologies based on
character-defining attributes such as building height, style, year of construction, or location
within the urban-to-rural continuum. A building typology also can be based on uses (for
example, residential, commercial, educational, religious), sustainability practices (for exam-
ple, water and energy efficiency, location efficiency), or renovation status (for example, fully
refurbished, high renovation potential).
In an analysis of neighborhood attributes in British Columbia, Canada, a typology of six
neighborhood types spanned a continuum from rural to more urban (Teed et al., 2002). The
neighborhood typology included the following residential types:
Single-family houses (attributes: small footprints, pitched roofs, one to two stories)
Multifamily residential towers (attributes: 5 to 12 stories, subdivided living units, retail
use of the first floor)
Mixed-use residential/commercial development (attributes: ground-floor retail or
office uses, upper-floor residential use, three to five stories, rectangular and linear
footprints)
Neighborhood and building typologies can be documented on image boards with photo-
graphs, annotated street elevations and sections, and maps showing the locations of repre-
sentative buildings or neighborhoods within each type.
In addition to the influence of buildings (Figure 6-2), the character of the built envi-
ronment is influenced by circulation system configurations, land use combinations, the size,
placement, and design of outdoor open spaces (Figure 6-3), and construction materials.
Regionally distinctive construction materials include, for example, locally quarried stone or,
in glaciated regions, boulders deposited during the last ice age (Figure 6-4). Native and
naturalized vegetation can also contribute to a site’s unique identity.
Assessing existing neighborhood and community development patterns can inform
context-sensitive site planning and building design. Neighborhood “pattern books,” for
example, enable the design of new development that enhances the unique local character by
Assessing the Site’s Cultural and Historic Contexts 	169

-- 187 of 387 --

repeating the best qualities of building and site design in that community and geographic
region (Urban Design Associates, 2003).
Public places are cultural resources that are also considered in assessing the neighborhood
and community context. Community assets that contribute to a neighborhood’s character
and sense of place include parks, schools, libraries, and community centers. Public open
spaces can provide not only visual amenities and outdoor recreational opportunities but also
space for stormwater management and other functions of green infrastructure. Both indoor
and outdoor places, privately owned but open to the public, perform important social
functions that help define the character of a place.
6.3 	HISTORIC RESOURCES
Context-sensitive design is responsive to the history and needs of a particular place and time
(Unwin, 2003). Depending on the age, quality, and role of these places in the local, regional,
or national culture, some of these resources may have significant historic value. In the United
States, the National Historic Preservation Act (NHPA) affords legal protection to buildings,
bridges, and other structures registered on the list of nationally significant historic resources.
Policies aimed at protecting and restoring historic resources also exist at the state and
local levels.
Cultural resource inventories document the location, quality, and historic significance of
buildings and other human-made elements, as well as prior land uses (Figure 6-5). Historic
Figure 6-2 	Horizontal and
vertical rhythm of highly articu-

block in San Francisco,
California.

-- 188 of 387 --

resources include bridges, buildings, walls, signs, and other noteworthy structures or
neighborhoods built in previous eras. The historic significance of a structure or neighbor-
hood is based on several criteria, including age, quality, rarity, and representativeness (Ames
and McClelland, 2002). Historic districts may be mapped by local planning agencies as
zoning “overlay districts,” with more restrictive development controls (Case Study 6-1).

Assessing the Site’s Cultural and Historic Contexts 	171

-- 189 of 387 --

Cultural resources also include historic sites such as forts, battlefields, parks, and
archaeological artifacts. Archaeological finds, such as Native American artifacts, can have a
significant impact on project planning and implementation. (Talarico, 1998, p. 250):
Rules governing finds vary according to the locality, the size of the site, and who is
involved in the project. If the property is private, finds can be bulldozed, except
where there’s legislation that says otherwise. If there are ties to the federal govern-
ment, even if the project is only partially funded by a federal agency, the site falls
under the National Historic Preservation Act, administered by the Department of the
Interior. The property must then undergo a phase-one archaeological analysis, during
which test pits are dug. If anything of value is found, the act requires a more extensive
phase-two dig. A phase-three dig is a full-blown excavation.
Culturally distinct urban districts can be mapped to illustrate the site’s cultural context
(Figure 6-6). Notable buildings also may be photographed (Figure 6-7) and mapped (Figure
6-8). This information can help architects and urban designers craft context-sensitive designs
for infill and redevelopment. In communities with active historic preservation interests,
redevelopment proposals that are “out of scale” or in other ways not in keeping with the
site’s architectural context may be doomed to fail.

Sanborn maps, originally created in the United States for the fire insurance industry, are
field survey maps providing detailed historical information on building heights, footprints,
past and present uses, and construction materials (Sanborn, 1999). More than 750,000 his-
toric Sanborn maps have been donated to the U.S. Library of Congress (Sanborn, 1999).
Other, more recent Sanborn maps are commercially available as georeferenced vector or
raster GIS data layers.

6.4 	DEMOGRAPHICS
Understanding a site’s cultural and historic context includes knowing something about the
people who live in the neighborhood or the immediate vicinity. In the United States,
decennial census data are publicly available for GIS analysis at the census tract and block
group levels. Depending on the type of planning and design project being considered,
demographic data may be collected on a variety of household attributes (Figure 6-9),
including:
Population density, age, and ethnicity
Household size, income, and composition
These and other attributes are commonly used in market studies to inform the pro-
gramming of private- and public-sector projects. Community-level sustainability initiatives
are also beginning to measure various social, economic, and environmental indicators for
individual neighborhoods and districts (Figure 6-10). These online resources are additional

of a place. Source: City of

fine-grained data that can help site planners understand the site’s social and economic
contexts (Figure 6-11).
6.5 	ENVIRONMENTAL PERCEPTION
In a classic book on landscape perception, D. W. Meinig (1979) identifies 10 possible ways in
which our knowledge, experiences, and values influence our perceptions of land and
landscapes (Table 6-1). Each perspective is a product of the unique “lens” through which
each of us views the world. Maslow’s (1954) hierarchy of human needs suggests that basic
human needs must be satisfied before higher-level secondary and tertiary needs can be
Figure 6-9 	Map of employees per acre in Madison, Wisconsin’s downtown planning area. Source: City of Madison, Wisconsin.
176 	Site Analysis

-- 194 of 387 --

satisfied. Physical safety and security—along with food, clothing, and shelter—are the most
basic needs in the hierarchy. Yet, perceptions of safety and security vary among individuals,
depending on their age, gender, health, and other attributes.
Our ability to see, smell, taste, touch, and hear informs our perceptions of our sur-
roundings. A sensory approach to environmental education, for example, is an effective way
to help students understand cause-and-effect relationships between society and the envi-
ronment (Auer, 2008). For most people, perceptions of the built environment are formed
primarily through the sense of sight. Physical well-being is linked to environmental quality,
and noise and air quality are also important factors, in addition to visual quality (National
Center for Complementary and Alternative Medicine, 2005). Visual resource assessments for
Figure 6-10 	Partial map of neighborhoods and districts for which the city of Madison collects demographic data and indicators of
community health and sustainability. Source: City of Madison, Wisconsin.
Assessing the Site’s Cultural and Historic Contexts 	177

-- 195 of 387 --

site-planning purposes are concerned with both visibility and visual quality (Arriaza et al.,
2004; Daniel, 2001; Dramstad et al., 2006). The significance of each attribute depends on
context, of course, but also on the intended uses of the site.
6.5.1 	Visibility
Context plays a crucial role in project programming and site design. Land uses on adjoining
sites, for example, can influence the suitability of a site for certain uses and use intensities.
Good visibility to the site from adjacent streets and other off-site locations may reduce the
site’s suitability for residential uses. But good visibility to the site can enhance its suitability
for commercial uses, thereby potentially increasing property values and business income.
Certain types of residential development, however, may place a premium on privacy and
screening from off-site locations.
How a site will be used can also depend on what can be seen from the site (Figure 6-12).
The visibility of off-site features can be as important as the visibility of on-site features. In
urban environments, views from a site may be blocked by nearby buildings. But if the site is
on a hill or a waterfront, unobstructed off-site views can be dramatic. In rural environments,
a site that is heavily forested yet relatively flat limits views on and off the site. In contrast, in
landscapes with long, unobstructed views—as in mountainous terrain or on the open
plains—the visible distance from the site can be substantial.
Viewsheds, or areas visible from specific locations on the site, can be estimated in a GIS by
analyzing topographic data. On-site assessments are essential, however, because existing trees
and buildings may block views from some vantage points. Moreover, in temperate climates,
visibility to and from a site may vary seasonally. Deciduous trees and shrubs may provide very
Figure 6-11 	Graph of top
visitors’ activities in Madison,
Wisconsin. Source: City of
Madison, Wisconsin.
178 	Site Analysis

-- 196 of 387 --

little screening in winter, when the plants are dormant and leafless. A site assessment,
therefore, may document the views from multiple site vantage points (Figure 6-13).
Buildings also substantially influence visibility and visual quality in urban and suburban
settings. Consequently, restrictions on building heights in capital cities are efforts to maintain
the visual prominence of each city’s capitol building—a landmark and a symbol of the seat of
government. In Madison, Wisconsin, building heights are restricted within 1 mile (1.6

kilometers) of the state’s capitol building (Figures 6-14 and 6-15). In Washington, D.C.,
building heights are restricted along the major avenues created by the 1791 L’Enfant plan
(Gutheim, 1977).
A visibility (or viewshed) map graphically shows the locations that can be seen from a
single viewing point. A “frequency seen” map, in contrast, characterizes the locations that
can be seen from two or more viewing points (Computer Terrain Mapping, 1997). This
map can be created, within a GIS, by overlaying a series of one-point viewshed maps. A
value of 1 is assigned to the visible areas on each layer, and areas that are not visible are
assigned a value of 0. The viewshed maps are combined through a polygon overlay process,
and the attribute values for all layers are summed. For example, when five viewshed maps are
combined, the frequency seen or visibility values on the output layer could range from 0
(locations that are not visible from any of the five viewing points) to 5 (locations that are
visible from all five viewing points). Each visibility class can be depicted with a single color
and/or texture.
Figure 6-13 	The visible context for a project extends well beyond the site’s boundaries. Source: Land Design. Used with
permission.
