6.5.2 	Visual Quality
Visual quality is influenced by distinctive biophysical and cultural features, which may
include rock outcrops, water bodies, and specimen trees as well as stone walls and houses of
worship. The type, abundance, and arrangement of these natural and built features influence
the landscape’s scenic quality (Table 6-2), which can be assessed at the site as well as at
regional scales (Figure 6-16).
Two approaches have been used in assessing the visual quality of cultural landscapes. The
objectivist approach assumes that visual quality is an inherent landscape attribute, whereas
the subjectivist approach assumes that visual quality is in the eyes of the beholder (Lothian,
Figure 6-14 	Map of maximum building heights allowed for the preservation of views to the state of Wisconsin’s capitol building.
Source: City of Madison, Wisconsin.
Assessing the Site’s Cultural and Historic Contexts 	181

-- 199 of 387 --

1999). Although it is true that conceptions of beauty are influenced by an observer’s
knowledge and experience, there is ample evidence to identify broadly accepted design
principles for improving the visual quality of the built environment (Figure 6-17).
Aesthetic quality assessments by experts take into account an area’s scenic qualities, or
visible attributes, which include form, proportion, line, color, and texture. Participatory
assessments, in contrast, may engage a representative, often randomly selected, group of
individuals to provide their opinions on the visual quality of existing, as well as proposed,
places within the built environment. Typically, visual preferences are elicited for a carefully
selected set of photographic scenes. These “visual preference surveys” (VPS) can be an
effective tool for engaging the public in neighborhood planning decisions.

Water 	Clarity, dominating element
Color 	Variety, contrast, and harmony
Scarcity 	Distinctive or unusual within the region
Cultural Modifications 	Add favorably to visual variety and harmony
Note: Criteria are rated on a scale from 5 (highest) to 0 (lowest).

A related approach is to provide cameras to individuals and ask them to photograph
scenes in their communities that they particularly like or dislike. This technique, called
“photo participatory mapping” (PPM), can help designers understand how the environment
affects residents’ behavior, especially among the adolescents and young adults in the com-
munity (Dennis et al., 2009).
Prominent views to natural or cultural amenities can have social as well as economic value
in areas dependent upon tourism. Jakle (1987, p. 11) comments on the importance of visual
quality to tourism:
Tourists come as strangers with accompanying needs to orient to new places. What
they see is novel and it attracts attention accordingly. As they seek interest and pleasure
in their visual surroundings, they are sensitive to those aspects of landscape, to those
places, which portend interest and pleasure.
In mountainous landscapes (Figure 6-18), where visual quality is important to the local or
regional economy, the skyline contributes to memorable images of place. At the University
of Washington in Seattle, for example, the campus design responds to distant views of
Mount Rainier. Although this majestic mountain is over 50 miles (80.5 kilometers) to the
southeast, it is a prominent campus landmark. This visual linkage is strengthened by carefully
preserving the sight lines to this dormant volcano from major campus open spaces.
Visual quality is an important factor in site planning and design. The visual quality of the
site itself, as well as the visible off-site features, can play key roles in the success of real estate
development projects. Views to significant buildings, prominent mountains, and other
landmarks, for example, convey a strong sense of place (Figure 6-19). Conversely, unob-
structed views to a busy highway, or to a large, treeless parking lot or to an industrial site can

be a liability that reduces the site’s suitability for certain uses, especially residential
development.
Visibility and visual quality affect land use preferences and real estate value, and these
attributes should be carefully considered in development planning and design. Whether
positive or negative, views and vistas are important to assess. At the landscape or even
regional scale, the study area can be divided into a regular grid, with each grid cell rated for
visual quality using one of four ordinal classes (Anderson, 1980):
Very unique
Unique
Frequent
Common
The visual quality of smaller areas can be mapped, too, although rather than using grid-cell
mapping units, individual buildings and outdoor spaces can be evaluated for their visual quality.
The mapping units at these smaller scales need not be uniform in either size or shape.
6.5.3 	Noise and Air Pollution
A site’s suitability for its intended uses may be affected not only by what people can see on
the site, but also by what they can hear and smell. Noise in the built environment varies on
an hourly, daily, or seasonal basis and can have a significant impact on recreational
experiences outdoors. In The End of Nature, for example, McKibbon (1989) suggests that a
true wilderness experience includes freedom from the noise of distant chain saws. Envi-
ronmental perception has experiential implications in other settings. In urban environments,
most parks, plazas, and other significant outdoor places are successful—from a design per-
spective—when vehicles are minimized. That includes the absence of vehicle noise and
traffic-related air pollution (Figure 6-20).
Noise can be described in terms of sound intensity (perceived as loudness) and frequency
(perceived as pitch). Sound intensity is measured as sound pressure level (SPL) on a loga-
rithmic decibel (dB) scale. The intensity and duration of noise exposure determine the
potential for damage to the inner ear. Permanent hearing loss can result from chronic noise
exposures to 85 dB(A) or higher, on average, for an 8-hour period (Morata et al., 1993, pp.
245–254). Yet, 4 hours of noise exposure at 88 dB provides the same noise “dose” as 8 hours
at 85 dB (Clark and Bohne, 1999). Common noise sources and loudness levels, measured in
decibels, are as follows (Rabinowitz, 2000, pp. 2749–2757):
Jet airplane takeoff (140)
Chain saw (110–120)
Stereo headphones (100)
Motorcycle (90)
Conversation (60)
Whisper (30–40)
Assessing the Site’s Cultural and Historic Contexts 	187

-- 205 of 387 --

The U.S. Occupational Safety and Health Administration (OSHA) sets noise standards
for the workplace. Hearing protection is recommended for anyone exposed to 85 dB or
higher, especially if the exposure is for a prolonged time.
According to the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), noise is
becoming an increasing public health concern (www.cdc.gov/nceh/hsb/noise/). Not only
can noise pollution cause hearing loss, it can also lead to other human health and devel-
opment problems. When traffic and other noises disturb sleep, for example, the impacts may
include diminished reading and language acquisition skills in children (Hendrick, 1997, p.
3G). Persistent traffic noise can also lead to prolonged stress, which has pernicious impacts on
the immune system and long-term health (Passchier-Vermeer and Passchier, 2000).
6.6 	CONCLUSION
Cities are complex mosaics of natural and built elements of varying ages and origins (Lynch,
1976). Cultural and historic resources, either on or near a site, can significantly influence the
site’s character and sense of place. And depending on the program for the site, these resources
may provide opportunities to create great new places through context-sensitive design.

heavy vehicle traffic are
sources of noise and poor air
quality.

Case Study 6–1 	Downtown Planning
DOWNTOWN PLAN
MADISON , WISCONSIN
URBAN DESIGN : CITY OF MADISON D EPARTMENT OF PLANNING , MADISON , WISCONSIN
PROJECT GOALS
In his 1911 Madison: A Model City (Figure 6-21), renowned landscape architect and city planner John Nolen
(p. 20) wrote about Madison’s unique character, which results, in part, from its location on an isthmus between
two lakes (Figure 6-22):
Its topography, its lake scenery, its early selection as the Capital and as the seat of the State University, its
population, its history, such influential factors as these should surely have found expression in a city plan, a
city development and a city life with the form and flavor unlike that of any other place.
On the 100th anniversary of this seminal document that subsequently shaped Madison’s development, the
city’s Department of Planning developed—with extensive input from an ad hoc multidisciplinary team of design
professionals—an updated Downtown Plan to guide the area’s transformation (and, where appropriate, historic
preservation). The plan builds on a rich planning tradition to provide a framework for the next 20 years. The
Downtown Plan will guide the future growth of the dynamic urban core while sustaining the traditions, history,
and vitality that have made Madison a model city.
Madison’s Downtown is the geographic, economic, and civic heart of the community. Downtown is the place
where the community comes together, especially for the many cultural and sporting events that it hosts and the
abundant educational and recreational activities that it provides. An active and healthy downtown is important
in ensuring the vitality of the rest of the community and region. And Downtown Madison has experienced a
renaissance in the 20 years since the last downtown planning effort. This new plan proposes a framework to
enhance the qualities that make Madison a world-class city while effectively addressing today’s challenges and
anticipating the needs of the next two decades and beyond.
AUTHENTIC SENSE OF PLACE
The Downtown Plan’s vision is grounded in a comprehensive analysis of the Downtown’s natural, cultural, and
historic resources. The City of Madison Comprehensive Plan states (2006, p. 2):
Ensuring that Downtown possesses an authentic sense of place—one that builds on its unique qualities,
reflects the values of the city’s residents, and does not strive to replicate other communities—is crucial in
ensuring its long term health.
Assessing the Site’s Cultural and Historic Contexts 	189

-- 207 of 387 --

Case Study 6–1 	Downtown Planning (continued)
Figure 6-21 	Madison: A Model City, a plan developed over 100 years ago by John Nolen, still influences the city’s built
environment. Source: City of Madison, Wisconsin.
190 	Site Analysis

-- 208 of 387 --

Case Study 6–1 	Downtown Planning (continued)
Downtown’s location on a narrow stretch of land between two lakes, coupled with its rolling topography,
provides a dramatic natural setting. In Madison: A Model City, John Nolen (1911, p. 20) wrote:
No other city of the world, so far as I know, has such a unique situation on a series of lakes with an
opportunity for so much and such direct relationship to beautiful water frontages. The physical situation
certainly is distinctly individual.
The highest point on the isthmus was chosen as the location for the State Capitol building. The Downtown
was designed to emphasize the prominence of this iconic civic structure, with panoramic views of it from
multiple vantage points, both near and far (Figure 6-23). The topography generally slopes down from the
Capitol to the lakes (Figure 6-24).

Monona. Source: City of Madison, Wisconsin.

Case Study 6–1 	Downtown Planning (continued)
Mansion Hill is located on a high ridge between the Capitol and Lake Mendota (Figure 6-25). Another
prominent hill adjacent to the planning area was chosen for the University of Wisconsin’s most important
building, Bascom Hall. The campus, with direct access to Lake Mendota, is a catalyst for Downtown redevel-
opment and an amenity providing cultural, educational, and recreational experiences (Figure 6-26).
In addition to being the capital of Wisconsin, Madison is the largest community in the region and Downtown
is located at its center. Downtown is the place where residents of the greater Madison region come together.
Madison’s near east and near west sides are home to strong residential neighborhoods, important community

Look and you will find it—what is unsought will go undetected.
—Sophocles
7.1 	INTRODUCTION
The human mind can comprehend the past and present, and speculate on the implications of
future actions (Ackoff, 1989) (Figure 7-1). In the site-planning process, information about
the past, present, and future is portrayed graphically. Graphic communication helps clients,
consultants, and other stakeholders understand—and participate in—the programming,
planning, and design process. Mapping, modeling, and other visualization techniques help to
communicate contextual conditions and propose design alternatives (Table 7-1).
Whether drawn by hand or with a computer, symbols are used alone and in combination
to convey information. Diagrams simplify reality and allow planners and designers to depict
relevant patterns and processes. Edward T. White (1983, p. 1) comments:
We designers are often more comfortable and skilled at drawing plans, elevations, sec-
tions, and perspectives than at diagramming project needs, issues, and requirements. We
sometimes seem overly anxious to draw the architectural answers to ill-defined project
questions and reluctant to invest in graphic techniques that help us better understand the
project needs and that stimulate responsive and creative design concepts.
Diagrams also can communicate desired relationships between existing and proposed off-
site and on-site elements. This is especially helpful in explaining to clients, colleagues, and
public sector reviewers why a design proposal is appropriate for the site.
204

-- 222 of 387 --

7.2 	ON-SITE DESIGN INFLUENCES
Site analysis goes well beyond mapping the site’s existing conditions. The site analysis is a
diagnostic process that identifies opportunities and constraints for specific land uses. The site
inventory provides the physical, biological, and cultural data for this program-driven analysis
(Figure 7-3).
Some locations on the site may be unsuitable for development because of intrinsic
physiographic constraints (Table 7-2). These constraints may include steep slopes, shallow
bedrock, surface water, and wetlands. Other parts of the site may be suitable for develop-
ment but are relatively inaccessible. Lack of access may be due to intervening site constraints

(Figure 7-3). Consequently, pockets of undevelopable land can render the original devel-

to isolated areas of the site. Gaining a better understanding of the site’s development lim-
itations is a common reason for revising the project’s program.
Design influences also include existing on-site features that are retained, undisturbed, in
the site plan. A steep wooded slope, for example, is a natural feature that contributes to the
site’s unique sense of place. Natural features can be viewed as development constraints that, if
disturbed, would have detrimental environmental or community impacts (Table 7-3). Yet,
these features also create opportunities to integrate green space—or green infrastructure—
into the site plan (Figure 7-4).
Ecological linkages between the site and the surrounding landscape can be strengthened
by protecting—and restoring—green infrastructure (United States Environmental Protec-
tion Agency, 2011). These multifunctional green spaces not only have significant ecological
value but can also provide recreational and educational opportunities, add aesthetic amenity,
and enhance property values.

A landscape’s green or ecological infrastructure includes open water, wetlands, aquifer
recharge areas, steep topography, and unique landforms. These environmental resources,
typically comprising a network of patches and corridors, warrant protection (Lewis,
1996). Moreover, this green infrastructure—which provides a wide range of ecological
services—can also serve as a spatial framework for shaping future growth and redevel-
opment (Figure 7-5).
7.2.1 	Difficult Sites
Building in difficult locations requires more time and money before, during, and after
construction. Steep slopes, for example, are a significant constraint for many developed uses
Figure 7-3 	The site analysis

Constraint 	Examples
Ecological Infrastructure 	Aquifer recharge areas, wetlands, surface water, critical wildlife habitat

(Figure 7-6). Building foundations constructed on steep slopes are more complex—stepping
down a hillside—and are, therefore, more expensive to design and build. In locations where
site drainage is poor or where the subsoil is unstable, additional effort is needed to ensure the
building’s structural integrity.
Building on difficult sites also increases the risks of development impacts both on-site and
off-site. Acquiring development permits for difficult sites is complicated, therefore, by the
potential impacts of the construction process as well as the postconstruction uses of the site.
Environmental impacts can include soil erosion, groundwater contamination, and the
degradation of critical wildlife habitat. Consequently, sites with significant physiographic
constraints may be economically—and politically—unfeasible to develop.

the spatial organization of program
elements on the site.

7.2.2 	Natural Hazards
Ignoring potential site hazards can lead to expensive—even deadly—disasters. Earthquakes,
hurricanes, and other natural hazards occur at infrequent intervals. The recurring nature of
these events suggests, however, that it is not a question of if, but when, an event will occur in
areas at risk. Earthquakes, for example, are dangerous geological hazards that impose shaking
forces on buildings, bridges, utility lines, and other structures. Risk can be mitigated through
innovative design and construction techniques (Dowrick, 1991; Erdey, 2007). Yet, when
the risk can be reduced significantly—for example, by not building in the most seismically
active area—this is clearly a prudent course of action.
Figure 7-5 	Ecological framework plan to protect environmental resources and shape new urban growth. Source: JJR SmithGroup.
SmithGroupJJR.
210 	Site Analysis

-- 228 of 387 --

Figure 7-6 	Site analysis
showing development con-
straints: drainage patterns, rid-
ges, and areas with slope
gradients greater than 20 per-
cent. This analysis also identifies
potential site access points to a
future industrial park in Onslow
County, North Carolina. Source:
Land Design. Used with
permission.
Integration, Synthesis, and Analysis 	211

-- 229 of 387 --

Natural hazards that may impact a site are often weather related. During periods of good
weather, the dangers may be absent, but the danger is greatest when relatively infrequent,
but severe, weather events do occur. Hurricanes, for example, are weather events with
significant land use planning implications. Hurricanes develop over water, so the risk they
pose is greatest in coastal communities. And because hurricanes are long-lasting storms, their
persistent rains can saturate soils and exacerbate the threat of local flooding. Advances in
building construction methods have reduced the risk of structural failures from storm wind
loads (Simiu and Miyata, 2006). But a hurricane’s main threat to life and property is storm
surge flooding (H. John Heinz Center for Science, Economics and the Environment, 2000).
Storm surges associated with hurricanes create walls of seawater that are at least several
feet (meters) above normal high-tide elevations. Development in low-lying areas is espe-
cially vulnerable to the devastating social and economic impacts of coastal flooding.
Moreover, the mean sea level is slowly rising and could rise up to (1 meter) over the next
century as the earth’s climate continues to change (Mileti, 1999).
Flooding is exacerbated in watersheds where building rooftops, streets and parking lots,
and other impervious land cover substantially reduce the capacity of the landscape to retain
and infiltrate stormwater. Managing the risks associated with climate change includes dis-
couraging development (and redevelopment) in high-risk areas (H. John Heinz Center for
Science, Economics and the Environment, 2002). It also includes increasing the capacity of
upland areas to infiltrate stormwater runoff.
7.3 	OFF-SITE DESIGN INFLUENCES
You can tell a lot about a city by looking.
—Jacobs (1985)
Off-site cultural and biophysical features can also influence the organization of the site
and the design of its new buildings and landscape. The number and location of entrances to
the site, for example, are determined, in part, by the transportation infrastructure just outside
the site’s boundaries. Adjoining streets and nearby transit stops, for example, may suggest
preferred locations for new site and building entrances. Corner sites at the intersection of
two major streets, for example, warrant special design treatment. A new building’s primary
entrance may be located at the corner, and the building facade that faces the intersection may
be rounded to “turn” the corner (Figure 7-7).
Land use regulations may also affect these decisions by limiting the number of vehicle
entrances, or “curb cuts,” to a site, imposing a minimum distance requirement between
vehicle entrances and nearby street intersections, and regulating the placement of new
buildings in relation to property lines and the public right-of-way. Views to prominent off-
site topographic features, trees, or water bodies also warrant context-responsive site planning
and design. Other major off-site design influences include local and regional architecture.
For example, prevalent building styles or materials may be “echoed” in new buildings and
site designs. An off-site landmark could also influence the siting of a new building to give the
building’s occupants a prominent view of the nearby landmark.
212 	Site Analysis

-- 230 of 387 --

Microclimate can vary dramatically over short distances in urban environments. Build-
ings, for example, can enclose outdoor areas and shelter these spaces from the wind. In
cooler climates, these pockets of calm air—especially if they receive direct sunlight—can
have much milder microclimates than nearby windy or shaded locations. These milder
microclimates provide opportunities to create meaningful outdoor open spaces.
Conversely, relatively harsh and uncomfortable microclimates also exist in urban areas.
Tall buildings and groups of buildings may channel winds to create turbulent windy areas. A
lack of exposure to sunshine can make these outdoor spaces substantially cooler than nearby
locations with greater exposure to the sun and protection from the wind. The site analysis
helps designers understand the site’s existing and, potentially, future microclimates.
7.3.1 	Placelessness
Landscape transformations—most significantly since the end of World War II—have
resulted from the interplay of many social, economic, and technological factors. Land use
changes have been influenced by the rise of the global economy and by public policies
affecting a range of activities, from transportation and economic development to school
siting (Ben-Joseph and Szold, 2005; Tiesdell and Adams, 2011). Construction methods and
materials also have become more internationally standardized, including in Europe (Scottish
Executive, 2003, p. 10):
Figure 7-7 	Context-sensitive
building design responds to
circulation patterns, such as
major pedestrian and vehicular
intersections. Washington, D.C.
Integration, Synthesis, and Analysis 	213

-- 231 of 387 --

Many suburban areas lack character, identity or variety. Too many new homes look as
if they could be anywhere. Thoughtlessly chosen standard housing types and inap-
propriate materials look disconcertingly out of place.”
Urban areas are complex social, economic, and ecological environments, and in this
context, site analyses must examine more than just physiographic conditions (Figure 7-8).
Contextual analysis can entail multiple lines of inquiry, including identifying neighborhood
and community spaces and determining what activities and symbolic values are associated
with those spaces. Analyses may also examine how—and why—people use, or avoid,
outdoor spaces. Postoccupancy evaluations of outdoor spaces are particularly useful in
designing urban sites (Cooper-Marcus and Francis, 1998).
The spatial and temporal qualities of places are particularly relevant to urban in-fill and
redevelopment projects. Jakle (1987, pp. 4–5) comments on the time and space dimensions
of place:
Certainly, places as behavioral settings have spatial context. They also, however, have
temporal dimensions because they open and close at set points in time and, thus,
function for set durations of time, often with cyclical regularity. They are occupied by
people (usually a limited range of types), by activities (usually a limited set of general
behaviors), and by a limited array of furnishings supportive of those behaviors.
Figure 7-8 	Site analysis showing prominent views, pedestrian–vehicle conflicts, cultural amenities, and a variety of other site and
contextual information. Source: Edward D. Stone, Jr., and Associates. Used with permission.
214 	Site Analysis

-- 232 of 387 --

To combat placelessness, careful attention must be given to creating meaningful open
spaces, to their spatial organization and connectivity, and to their furnishings. Pedestrian
circulation within, and surrounding, the site should be analyzed to identify opportunities
for creating entrance plazas and other pedestrian amenities. This analysis should also look for
problems such as the following:
Lack of walkway connectivity
Insufficient walkway capacity
Conflicts among vehicles, bicycles, and pedestrians
Lack of street trees and other plantings
Insufficient or excessive outdoor lighting
Lack of site furniture
A comprehensive neighborhood revitalization program would view these problems as
potential opportunities to improve the neighborhood’s livability and sense of place.
The site’s boundary—or interface with adjoining properties and transportation infra-
structure—is a particularly important site-planning concern. The periphery or edge of the
site takes on greater importance when the site is small or linear in shape. Potential conflicts
can exist between on-site and off-site activities, and these must be addressed during the
analysis and conceptual design phases (Figure 7-9). Off-site conditions can include nuisances,
such as a busy highway, which impact the site with traffic noise, odors, and potential safety
Figure 7-9 	Diagram of waterfront districts in Cleveland, Ohio, and potential opportunities for coordinated planning and design.
Source: JJR SmithGroup. 	SmithGroupJJR.
Integration, Synthesis, and Analysis 	215

-- 233 of 387 --

risks. When conflicts do exist, programmed elements can be arranged to mitigate them. Uses
that are less sensitive to these nuisances, for example, can be sited closer to them than other,
more sensitive, uses. In either case, screening and buffering may be needed, but the selection
of screening materials—which can include walls, fences, berms, and/or vegetation—occurs
later in the design development process.
7.3.2 	Sense of Place
A site analysis focuses on more than just site constraints, although this alone is enough to
justify the effort. Amenities on-site and off-site may have significant social, economic,
ecological, or aesthetic value. These can include specimen trees or scenic views to natural
features such as water or landforms, landmark buildings, or other major cultural features
(Figures 7-10 and 7-11). Amenities also include unique rock outcrops and historic or cul-
turally significant buildings and structures (Table 7-4). These physical assets, if integrated
within a redevelopment plan, can maintain a site’s sense of place and enhance experiences for
future site users. Protecting natural and cultural amenities can also increase a property’s value
and make it a more desirable place to be.
Site opportunities include locations that are potentially usable for one or more project
objectives—whether they entail real estate development, environmental conservation or
restoration, or a combination of these purposes (Figure 7-12). Together, the site’s constraints
and opportunities delimit the area of the site that can accommodate the project program
(Figure 7-13). Like putting together a jigsaw puzzle, design solutions that fit the site often
elegantly capitalize on the site’s natural and cultural attributes, creating a unique sense of place.
A site’s biophysical and cultural context reflects local, community, and regional influ-
ences. Michael Hough (1990, p. 180) comments on the biophysical and cultural complexity
of regional context:
Regional identity is connected with the peculiar characteristics of a location. . . . It
has to do, therefore, with two fundamental criteria: first, with the natural processes of
the region or locality—what nature has put there; second, with social processes—what
people have put there.
Hough (1990, p. 186) continues:
The protection of natural and cultural history—the reuse and integration of the old
into the new without fanfare, while avoiding the temptation to turn everything into a
museum because it is old—lies at the heart of maintaining a continuing link with the
past and with a place’s identity.
Contemporary references to earlier design styles, construction methods, and materials
create linkages to the site’s cultural history and environmental character (Figure 7-14). A
contextual approach to site planning and design is a land use ethic that seeks to create
meaningful places that are also “good neighbors.” Buildings are constructed and sites are
developed in ways that are sensitively adapted to both the historical and contemporary
contexts (Figure 7-15).

Natural as well as built features give communities and neighborhoods unique identities.
Morrish and Brown (2000), in Planning to Stay, suggest that neighborhood character is a
product of the quality, accessibility, and convenience of the place. Character is derived from
the neighborhood’s location within the larger community, its mix and arrangement of land
uses, and the scale of its buildings, streets, and open spaces.
An important objective of the site analysis is to discover the special, interesting, and
valuable features on and near the site. These assets can range from unique natural areas to
culturally significant local places that—to someone unfamiliar with the neighborhood or
community—may be overlooked. Neighborhood character analyses may involve, therefore,
assessments of the following elements (Morrish and Brown, 2000):
Homes and gardens
Community streets
Neighborhood niches
Anchoring institutions
Figure 7-13 	Opportunities and constraints diagram to guide the expansion of a historic village. Fenton, Missouri. Source: The
HOK Planning Group. Used with permission of The HOK Planning Group.
220 	Site Analysis

-- 238 of 387 --

A comprehensive photographic record of these character-defining elements can be arranged
on an image-board. This can serve as a reference during the design phases and as supporting
documentation during the permitting and approval process.
Other off-site design influences may include nearby prominent landmarks, including
iconic buildings, as well as the spaces linking these buildings. An analysis of a neighborhood’s
architectural context might document the styles and characteristic details of the neighbor-
hood’s better-designed buildings (Urban Design Associates, 2003). A religious building such
as a church or cathedral or a well-designed school or library could, therefore, influence the
siting and design of new buildings, nearby.
The Capitol District in Washington, D.C., for example, is replete with historic buildings
and national monuments that are located in a clearly organized geometric configuration
(Newton, 1971). L’Enfant’s eighteenth-century plan for the nation’s capital established axial
relationships between prominent landmarks, creating interlocking open spaces that to this
day form a visibly cohesive urban milieu. The “shaft of space,” visually linking prominent
buildings and other cultural elements, is a classic organizational device in context-sensitive
Figure 7-14 	Composite set of site inventory and analysis maps in Lavasa, India. Source: The HOK Planning Group. Used with
permission of The HOK Planning Group.
Integration, Synthesis, and Analysis 	221

-- 239 of 387 --

urban design (Bacon, 1974, pp. 70–71). In Design of Cities, Edmund Bacon explains the
historic use of this technique in shaping the urban environment.
7.4 	LAND USE SUITABILITY ANALYSIS
In Design with Nature, McHarg (1969) argued that environmental conditions should prin-
cipally inform land use planning decisions. McHarg’s book brought map overlays and
suitability analysis to the attention of many land planners and environmental scientists. Since
the time of McHarg’s seminal contributions to the field, GIS hardware and software have
been dramatically improved, making suitability analysis feasible for site planning at any scale.
Suitability analysis is “the process of determining the fitness, or the appropriateness, of a
given tract of land for a specified use” (Steiner, 1991, p. 132). Suitability analysis is spatially
Figure 7-15 	The master plan protects existing forests and drainageways, fits new development into the resulting spaces, and
restores degraded riparian vegetation. Source: The HOK Planning Group. Used with permission of The HOK Planning Group.
222 	Site Analysis

-- 240 of 387 --

explicit and program dependent. And because land development can create significant off-
site impacts that impose economic and social costs on others, context-sensitive site planning
seeks to minimize these detrimental impacts, or externalities.
Site suitability, therefore, is a function of the site’s intended uses. Consider a partially
wooded and hilly site along a small stream. Several site attributes could be mapped, but not
all of these attributes will be relevant to the program under consideration. The site attributes
most relevant to a proposed nature conservancy, for example, would differ from the site
attributes most relevant to a proposed multifamily housing development. The analysis for the
nature conservancy project would place a high priority on identifying the site’s plant
communities and wildlife habitats—from dry upland forested slopes to lowland marshes and
bogs. In contrast, the analysis for the housing project would place a high priority on
identifying areas without significant building constraints, which include steep slopes, shallow
bedrock, and a shallow water table.
Rather than using brute force engineering to overcome the intrinsic difficulties that some
sites pose for development, many unsuitable locations should simply remain undeveloped.
Refraining from developing in unique natural areas can be justified from an ecological and
ethical perspective, and often can also be supported from an economic perspective. Pro-
tecting and integrating this green infrastructure into the built environment can, when done
well, increase community livability and property values while also reducing public expen-
ditures for “big pipe” stormwater management infrastructure (Figure 7-16).
Residential subdivision design, therefore, involves much more than the efficient layout of
lots, streets and utilities. Typically, the most profitable—and aesthetically pleasing—plan is
one that takes advantage of the natural features on-site and off-site and makes these features
neighborhood focal points (Peiser, 1992, p. 71). Developing “with nature” is also a fiscally
Figure 7-16 	A brute force engineering approach to conveying urban stormwater runoff as quickly as possible to the nearest
detention pond—or worse—to a wetland, river, or other water body.
Integration, Synthesis, and Analysis 	223

-- 241 of 387 --

prudent approach for the municipalities in which the development occurs. Development
that respects inherent environmental constraints, especially natural hazards, helps to protect
public health, safety, and welfare. This development paradigm also celebrates each site’s
unique sense of place.
On relatively large sites, especially where built elements will cover a small fraction of the
parcel’s surface area, suitability analysis is an important step in the land planning process.
Typically, this means assessing the site’s suitability for development, which involves the
construction of roads and buildings. In rural areas without municipal services, development
suitability may also depend on the soil’s capacity to accommodate on-site wastewater
treatment systems or support special agricultural uses, such as orchards and vineyards (see
Case Study 7-1). Sites that are most suitable for agricultural use require fewer management
inputs. Locations with prime soils, for example, require comparatively less irrigation, less
fertilizers and pesticides, and less effort and expense devoted to erosion control.
A land use suitability analysis involves the following steps:
1. Identify the suitability criteria for each anticipated land use.
2. Inventory and map the relevant site attribute data.
3. Identify and map the locations with attribute values that meet the suitability criteria
for the targeted land uses.
Before beginning a suitability analysis, both the availability and reliability of the required
data should be considered (Anderson, 1980; Pease and Sussman, 1994). For example, do the
data exist at the needed scale and can they be affordably acquired? Are the data current, and
do they pertain to the suitability criteria for the proposed uses?
A suitability analysis may assess several cultural and biophysical attributes. If the analysis is
conducted within a GIS, with attribute maps in digital form, then suitability maps can be
easily generated for each proposed land use. These maps can then be combined to create—
on a single map—a synoptic suitability analysis for the proposed project.
7.4.1 	Single-Attribute Analysis
Data Partitioning
A GIS database consists not only of maps, but also of data tables that are linked to the points,
lines, and polygons on the maps (Chrisman, 1997). Location and attribute information for
each map unit is stored as tabular data. The analysis of an individual attribute layer involves
partitioning the spatial distribution of these attribute values. The primary objective usually is
to find the locations that are either greater than a specified minimum, less than a specified
maximum, or within a specified range. These conditions create suitable—or unsuitable—
places for one or more components of the proposed program.
A map showing the site’s range of slope percentages (for example, 0–5 percent or 5–10
percent) is an example of spatial partitioning. This slope map is useful because some land uses
are better suited for gentle slopes, and other uses are better suited for steeper slopes. In gently
rolling to nearly level terrain, existing slopes may have little effect on land planning and site
design decisions. In steeper terrain, however, elevation, slope, and aspect influence the site’s
hydrology, visual quality, and plant and animal distributions (Figures 7-17, 7-18, and 7-19).

Spatial Buffering
Spatial buffering, or proximity analysis, identifies locations within a specified distance of one
or more reference features. The resulting areas are either buffers or setbacks; the primary
difference between a buffer and a setback is the direction (outward or inward) from the
reference feature (Figure 7-20).
Buffer analysis is used to identify locations requiring protection from development or
locations posing significant risks to human life and property. These include:
Natural resources (for example, streams, wellheads, wetlands, habitats of endangered
or threatened species)
Cultural resources requiring protection from development (for example, historic
buildings and districts)
Natural hazards (for example, coastal storm surges, landslides).
Buffer analyses can identify potential components of a green infrastructure system.
Maintaining adequate plant cover within riparian areas and other buffer zones can signifi-
cantly limit erosion, sedimentation, and pollution of aquatic ecosystems by filtering
stormwater surface runoff. Riparian buffers can also protect important terrestrial habitats and
climate adaptive migration corridors.

and setbacks within a GIS. Source:

Wiley & Sons, Inc.

7.4.2 	Multiple-Attribute Analysis
Although complex spatial analyses are possible with a digital GIS, a small number of ana-
lytical functions are most useful for site-planning purposes. A site suitability analysis typically
involves overlaying two or more attribute layers (Figure 7-21). The intersection and union
analyses are two of the most common and useful algebraic functions performed on attribute
layers. For a comprehensive review of these GIS overlay operations, see Chrisman (1997).
Union
The union of two sets of numbers yields a third set that contains each unique number in the
two original sets as follows:
1, 3 	2, 3 	1, 2, 3
The union function in a GIS identifies locations where one or more of the specified attri-
butes occur. For example, the analyst might want to identify all areas that pose severe
constraints for excavation and the construction of building foundations. Attributes that could
hinder excavation or construction include shallow depth-to-water-table and shallow depth-
to-bedrock. An overlay analysis, using the union function, could identify all site areas that
meet either one or both of these conditions.
Intersection
The intersection of two sets of numbers yields a third set of numbers that are common to
both original sets as follows:
1, 3 	2, 3 	3
Figure 7-21 	Overlay analysis
using a linear combination
approach. Source: Exploring
Geographical Information
Systems by N. Chrisman.
Copyright 	1997 by John
Wiley & Sons, Inc. Reprinted by
permission of John Wiley &
Sons, Inc.
228 	Site Analysis

-- 246 of 387 --

This intersection function enables an overlay analysis to identify locations where two or
more attribute conditions overlap or are spatially coincident. An intersection of two or more
attribute layers could yield, for example, site locations with slope gradients of less than 8
percent and subsurface conditions suitable for the excavation of building foundations.
Caution
A GIS is a powerful tool for spatially analyzing a site’s physical, biological, and cultural
attributes. Yet, a GIS is capable of easily comparing “apples and oranges”—even when these
comparisons can lead to erroneous conclusions (Chrisman, 1997; Hopkins, 1977).
Weighting schemes are frequently used to assign priority values to key site attributes. And
although it is mathematically possible to weight each attribute and arrive at an algebraic sum,
the significance of the values may be questionable.
The U.S. Department of Agriculture’s Land Evaluation and Site Assessment (LESA)
decision-support system helps local and state governments protect prime and other locally
important farmland from development (Steiner et al., 1994). The LESA system has been
used to determine the boundaries of agricultural zoning districts, identify farms that are
eligible for purchase of development rights (PDR) or transfer of development rights (TDR),
and for other land use policy objectives (Malloy and Pressley, 1994, pp. 262–273).
One criticism of the LESA system, as it was implemented in the early 1990s, is that it
conflated two disparate policy issues within one aggregate index (Pease and Sussman, 1994,
pp. 94–105). The LESA index attempted to assess agricultural productivity as well as
potential for development. These two disparate land uses should have been evaluated sep-
arately. Another criticism was data redundancy, because the suitability analysis was dispro-
portionately influenced by individual land use constraints that were represented by multiple
data themes (Pease and Sussman, 1994, pp. 94–105). The greatest challenge in using
weighting schemes is to identify weights that can be justified empirically. For example, a site
analyst may consider visual quality, slope gradients, and soil stability to be the most important
variables in assessing a particular site’s suitability for residential development. But how
much weight should be placed on each variable? One could assign, for example, a 15 percent
weight to visual quality, a 25 percent weight to soil stability, and a 60 percent weight to slope
gradient. These are not trivial decisions; weighting choices can dramatically affect the
conclusions of the analysis.
7.5 	SUITABILITY AND DEVELOPMENT REGULATION
The State Growth Management Act, passed in Washington State in 1990, requires local
municipalities to adopt critical areas ordinances that protect wetlands, aquifer recharge areas
(for potable water sources), fish and wildlife habitat conservation areas, frequently flooded
areas, and geologically hazardous areas (Marks, 1997). The Environmentally Critical Areas
Policies for Seattle states (Marks, 1997, pp. 230–231):
The Critical Areas Ordinance should allow land to be developed in accordance with
the constraints and opportunities provided by the land itself. All land is not the same.
If a person purchases a parcel that is 80 percent wetland, it is significantly different than
Integration, Synthesis, and Analysis 	229

-- 247 of 387 --

other types of property. The same is true with areas subject to either landslides or
floods. If the owner purchased property that contained a wetland, a landslide area, or a
flood plain, the Critical Areas Ordinance should recognize that the reasonable
development potential of such properties is less than the reasonable potential of
unconstrained sites. The ordinance should permit development that makes use of a
site’s natural opportunities and that recognizes its natural constraints.
Hazard maps and resource inventories created by state and federal governments are not
intended to support detailed site planning, and Seattle’s development regulations recognize
these data limitations. The city’s development controls are linked to the actual site conditions
(Table 7-5) rather than to constraint maps, as indicated in the following excerpt:
When specific actions are proposed in or adjacent to mapped critical areas, more
detailed review may be required. Projects located within a mapped area could be
exempted from the regulations, if the applicant can demonstrate that the site does not,
in fact, meet the definition of the critical area. Conversely, developments outside of
the mapped area could be covered by the regulations, if it is shown that the site does,
in fact, meet the definition (Marks, 1997, p. 231).
Also taking a context-responsive approach to regulating land development, a model
ordinance for traditional neighborhood development (TND) includes explicit criteria for

determining a site’s allowed capacity for new housing development (Arendt, 1999). Several
constraint categories, each with a different ratio, are used to compute the number of
dwelling units permitted on the developable areas of the site (Table 7-6). The area of land in
each constraint category is multiplied by that constraint’s development ratio and these values
are summed (Arendt, 1999). This number (total adjusted tract acreage) is then multiplied by
the dwelling density that is allowed in the zoning district. This results in the total number of
permitted dwellings (absent density bonuses) that can be built on the developable portions
of the site.
A qualitative approach to assessing development suitability is also possible. Nelesson
(1994), for example, categorizes constraints into three levels: highest, severe, and
moderate. Unlike some other approaches, which focus only on the site’s physiographic
limitations, Nelesson (1994, pp. 116–117) considers development opportunities as well as
constraints. These opportunities may be biophysical or cultural factors that potentially
enhance the success of the project from a social, economic, and/or environmental
perspective (Table 7-7).
A suitability analysis can be narrowly focused to evaluate locations for specific site
uses or activities (Figure 7-22). For example, most land development projects require
one or more vehicle entrances to the site. Site factors that could influence the entrance
location decision include the vertical and horizontal alignment of adjacent roads, site
amenities such as significant trees or landforms, and views from the site to the sur-
rounding landscape. Consequently, the suitability of potential entrance locations can be
analyzed, ranked, and mapped.

Integration, Synthesis, and Analysis 	231

-- 249 of 387 --

Suitability for a given land use can vary substantially with regional differences in climatic
conditions. For example, maximum feasible slope gradients for housing development will
vary with soil and geologic conditions and climate. Locations with subfreezing winter
temperatures are much more limiting because of the dangers that icy conditions create for
pedestrians and vehicles. The steep street gradients in San Francisco, California, for example,
would be impractical in a city with snowstorms and freezing winter temperatures. San
Francisco’s street system—primarily a grid configuration imposed on a hilly landscape—has
many street gradients between 20 and 30 percent. In many other cities, maximum street
gradients are typically 12 percent or less.
7.6 	CONCLUSION
Land development can result, both during and after construction, in externalities that
degrade the environment and impose substantial economic and social costs on others. Informed
by careful site and contextual analysis, however, new development can be designed to min-
imize these adverse impacts on the environment and the community. Development-
shaping site constraints can be shown diagrammatically on both the site analysis and
concept plan.
Each site presents a unique combination of physical, biological, and cultural conditions
that preclude a “one-size-fits-all” approach to its planning and design. Development suit-
ability depends on the site’s opportunities and constraints for the specific land use program
under consideration. Favorable site locations have attributes that are either essential for the
programmed uses or enable access to areas well-suited for these uses. Unfavorable locations
prevent these uses from occurring, or increase the difficulty or cost of siting the uses there.

Case Study 7–1 	Community Planning and Farmland Preservation
S OUTH L IVERMORE VALLEY
CALIFORNIA
LAND PLANNING : WALLACE , ROBERTS & T ODD , LLC, SAN F RANCISCO
ARCHITECTURE : TURNBULL GRIFFIN H AESLOOP A RCHITECTS , S AN FRANCISCO
BACKGROUND
South Livermore Valley is located in California’s rolling hills of Alameda County (Figure 7-23). This has been a
grape-growing and wine-producing area since the 1880s. As in other communities across North America, relatively
recent rural residential development began to significantly change this pastoral landscape. Conventional zoning
codes and subdivision ordinances were unable to protect the valley’s rural character and viticulture industry. A
participatory planning process, supported by a comprehensive analysis of the valley’s biophysical and cultural
conditions, informed a more sustainable approach for guiding new growth and development within the valley.
PLANNING PROCESS
An advisory committee was formed, consisting of concerned citizens and representatives from local munici-
palities. The 4-year consensus-building planning process, which began in 1987, ultimately resulted in the South
Figure 7-23 	The visual character of this California landscape is defined by hills and vineyards. Source: Wallace Roberts &
Todd, LLC. Used with permission.
234 	Site Analysis

-- 252 of 387 --

Case Study 7–1 	Community Planning and Farmland Preservation (continued)
Livermore Valley Specific Plan (City of Livermore, 1999). This plan set the framework for development in a
14,000-acre (5600-hectare) area. The two major goals of the plan are to reinforce and rejuvenate the valley’s
wine-producing industry (Figure 7-24) and to preserve the area’s rural character (Figure 7-25). Design guidelines,
based on historic regional precedents, have helped shape recent development patterns and building forms,
which differ markedly from those of the typical suburban tract development in the area. The newer context-
sensitive development reinforces the valley’s rural and historic character while forging a distinctive new identity
for the area’s future (Figure 7-26).
PLAN AND POLICY IMPLEMENTATION
Three strategies were adopted to accomplish the community’s goals.
1. Specific Plan. This land use plan targets new growth within seven distinct and noncontiguous subareas
along 6 miles (9.7 kilometers) of Livermore’s southern border (Figures 7-27 and 7-28). The total area allo-
cated for new development is about 1500 acres (600 hectares), or about 1/10th of the entire planning area.
Constraint maps and program-sensitive site analyses helped the planning team determine the preferred
character and location of all new development (Figures 7-29 and 7-30).
A “specific plan” in the state of California is a planning and regulatory tool to implement a city or county
general plan through policies, programs, and regulations (Governor’s Office of Planning and Research,
Figure 7-24 	Development under the plan permanently protects agricultural land and funds new vineyard and orchard
plantings. Source: Wallace Roberts & Todd, LLC. Used with permission.
Integration, Synthesis, and Analysis 	235

-- 253 of 387 --

Case Study 7–1 	Community Planning and Farmland Preservation (continued)
2001). In the case of Livermore, the specific plan provides a fine-grained response to questions about how,
where, and what development can take place. It includes detailed design guidelines to ensure consistency
with the community’s desired character—and to establish the means by which agricultural and natural
resources are conserved.
2. Incentives. The plan creates a density bonus to encourage compact development while protecting and
enhancing the valley’s agricultural operations. One new residential unit is allowed for each 100 acres (40
hectares), but four additional residential units are allowed if 90 percent of the 100 acres is legally protected
in perpetuity with agricultural easements, and these 90 acres (36 hectares) are planted with orchards or
vineyards. Unlike some other agricultural operations, orchards and vineyards can be economically viable in
relatively small areas.
3. Implementation. A nonprofit land trust, funded through development fees ($10,000 per unit), manages the
agricultural easements required by the plan. The land trust also buys additional easements to help achieve
the plan’s agricultural preservation goals.

DESIGN FEATURES
The plan limits the visual impact of new development on the valley’s rural, agricultural character. The plan also
maximizes open space amenities, including an extensive hiking trail network, which has benefited community
residents as well as visitors. Design guidelines ensure that conforming development patterns, details, and
materials enhance the valley’s historic wine country character (Figure 7-31). The new development has narrow
tree-lined streets with drainage swales (rather than curbs and gutters), short blocks, and traditional architectural
styles (Figure 7-32).
IMPLEMENTATION
Productive agricultural open space helps to define the visual character of new neighborhoods in South Liver-
more Valley. The community’s development mitigation program requires not only conservation easements to
protect agricultural land, but also new viticultural plantings. Bonding of those plantings, for 8 years, ensures
that the crops are established and properly maintained.
The South Livermore Valley Specific Plan received the 2006 National Award for Outstanding Implementation
from the American Planning Association (APA).

This final section of the book has two chapters that focus on context-sensitive site planning’s
form-giving phases. Chapter 8, “Conceptual Design,” concerns the spatial organization of

c h a p t e r 	8
Conceptual Site Design
Make no little plans; they have no magic to stir men’s blood and probably themselves will
not be realized. Make big plans; aim high in hope and work, remembering that a noble,
logical diagram once recorded will never die, but long after we are gone will be a living
thing, asserting itself with ever-growing consistency.
—Burnham (1893)
8.1 	INTRODUCTION
Design theory is a set of guiding principles and strategies for shaping the built environment.
Design theory addresses the decision-making process (for example, in site planning, conduct
the site analysis before embarking on conceptual design). And design theory also addresses
rules of thumb for spatially organizing program components and articulating those com-
ponents through more detailed context-sensitive design. These design principles are based
on a continually evolving body of knowledge informed by history, the arts, and the sciences.
The effective application of design theory, in site planning and architectural design, requires
creative problem solving—which, fortunately, can be fostered through education and
experience.
8.2 	CONTEXT-SENSITIVE DESIGN
Design with nature; design with culture; design for people.
This simple statement captures the essence of context-sensitive site planning. Respon-
siveness to on-site and off-site contexts takes into consideration a potentially complex set of
existing physical, biological, cultural, and legal conditions. Conceptual site design flows
247

-- 265 of 387 --

directly from the site analysis, which typically reveals biophysical, cultural, and regulatory
conditions that limit the number of feasible design alternatives (Figure 8-1).
Sites that have few, if any, significant biophysical or cultural constraints are often the most
challenging to design. The absence of significant constraints allows for a much wider range of
feasible options for organizing activities and designing structures on the site. The lack of site
character also makes it more challenging to create a unique sense of place. High-quality
building and site design can, however, create great places that—as on-site trees grow and
mature—become even better over time.
In contrast, significant natural or cultural features on or near a site have the potential to
inspire context-sensitive plans that fit the site. A site with a large, picturesque specimen tree,
for example, limits options for site development to locations that will protect the tree.
Another example is a stream on the site that restricts building options to higher elevations
Figure 8-1 	Conceptual design considers program, community goals, and site and contextual
conditions.
248 	Site Analysis

-- 266 of 387 --

that are not prone to flooding and, if developed, not likely to degrade the stream’s aquatic
and riparian habitats. In both scenarios, “designing with nature” produces context-sensitive
site plans that incorporate—rather than destroy—natural and cultural amenities.
Context-sensitive design responds creatively to the site’s existing conditions, rather than
forcing on the site generic predesigned templates for buildings, parking lots, and other
common site elements. Conditions that shape, or inform, the development of context-
sensitive site plans include both on-site (intrinsic) and off-site (extrinsic) factors. These design
influences, or determinants, provide the basis for organizing and articulating a project’s
program elements on the selected site.
The project’s objectives—or program—determine what is designed on the site (see
Chapter 2). These objectives range from private sector real estate development to public
sector nature conservation and restoration. On projects involving new buildings, the client
may have preferences regarding architectural style and surface finishes. On some projects, the
future users of the site may also play a significant role in influencing the site’s design. This is
particularly common with public sector projects, such as parks, libraries, and schools.
8.3 	DESIGN CREATIVITY
Problem solving is integral to the site-planning and design process. Site selection, for
example, is largely an optimization problem aimed at finding an available site that can best
satisfy the project’s objectives. Once a site is selected, identifying its constraints and
opportunities is an effort to understand the “puzzle” that the site poses for the program under
consideration. Solutions to this puzzle are sought during the conceptual design phase and, in
more detail, through subsequent design development. Donald Schoen, in Educating the
Reflective Practitioner, writes (1987, p. 42):
Designers juggle variables, reconcile conflicting values, and maneuver around con-
straints—a process in which, although some design products may be superior to
others, there are no unique, right answers.
Creative problem solving, as summarized by Kvashny (1982, p. 107), involves the pursuit
of five things: facts, problems, ideas, solutions, and acceptance (of the solutions). Each of
these is evident in the site-planning and design process. The programming and site inventory
phases, for example, involve fact finding. The site analysis assesses the site’s suitability for a
specific project program or set of objectives. This assessment identifies “problems” (con-
straints) and “ideas” (opportunities). Conceptual design and subsequent design refinement
generate “solutions” that seek to avoid or, in some cases, overcome the site’s constraints and
capitalize on the site’s opportunities. These solutions are communicated, evaluated, and—if
accepted by key stakeholders—approved and implemented.
An acceptable site plan meets the project objectives—as defined not only by the client,
but also by the community in which the site is located. Community goals are typically
expressed through comprehensive plans, zoning codes, and development review standards
and practices.
Site planning and design, therefore, involve judgments of relevance (for example, what
site data to collect) and of spatial relationships (for example, how the site would affect
Conceptual Site Design 	249

-- 267 of 387 --

a potential program activity). Site planning also involves acts of creating (for example,
arranging different land uses) and assessing the implications of those decisions (for exam-
ple, anticipating the design’s effects on pedestrians’ access, comfort, and safety).
Good design, according to Richard Seymour (Lawson, 2006, p.154), results from “the
unexpectedly relevant solution, not wackiness parading as originality.” In How Designers
Think, Brian Lawson (2006, p. 153) elaborates on the important distinction in design
between “originality” and “creativity” as follows:
In the competitive and sometimes rather commercial world of design, the novel and
startlingly different can sometimes stand out and be acclaimed purely for that reason.
But being creative in design is not purely or even necessarily a matter of being
original.
Robert Venturi, the noted architect, echoes this sentiment by commenting that—for a
designer—“it is better to be good than to be original” (Lawson, 2006, p. 154).
Creative designers find contextually appropriate—and sometimes even elegant—solu-
tions to design challenges. Again, Donald Schoen, writes (1987, p. 41):
Designing in its broader sense involves complexity and synthesis. In contrast to
analysts or critics, designers put things together and bring new things into being,
dealing in the process with many variables and constraints, some initially known and
some discovered through designing.
Fortunately, the ability to create appropriate design solutions can be nurtured. And one of
the best ways to become a better designer is to develop and refine one’s “design vocabulary.”
For decades, North American architects and landscape architects have traveled abroad to
enrich their understanding of urban design and to supplement their formal design training
(Fabos et al., 1968; Schulze, 1994). Relatively high population densities, long periods of
intensive urban refinement, and a strong public policy interest in urban design make
European cities living laboratories for architects, landscape architects, and urban designers
(Nivola, 1999). European cathedrals, plazas, parks, and waterfronts continue to be among
the special places sought out by foreign-educated designers.
The process of developing one’s design vocabulary is enhanced by careful observation—
often by keeping a sketchbook of notes and drawings, including perspective sketches, sec-
tions, elevations, and design details. Perhaps the most important reason to keep a sketch-
book, at least early in one’s design training, is that it fosters critical analysis—or the ability to
“read” the built environment. Once this skill is acquired, every built environment—elegant
or mundane—can be assessed with a discerning eye.
A comprehensive knowledge of design theory—its principles and varied forms of
expression, regardless of style—can be developed by critically analyzing relevant precedents.
Design precedents include both natural and built environments. Halprin’s studies of
waterfalls in California’s Sierra Nevada Mountains informed his designs of Portland’s public
fountains (Halprin, 1972). Precedent studies of the built environment, ranging from formal
postoccupancy evaluations to brief, informal meanderings through an unfamiliar city, crit-
ically examine the ways that places have been designed for specific purposes and in response
250 	Site Analysis

-- 268 of 387 --

to unique contextual conditions. Examples of great design—as well as spectacular design
failures—yield lessons that can strengthen the designer’s “toolkit” and capacity to develop
context-sensitive design solutions. Kneller (1965, p. 49) writes:
One of the paradoxes of creativity is that . . . we must familiarize ourselves with the
ideas of others. These ideas can then form a springboard from which the creator’s ideas
can be launched.
Many good designers, even seasoned practitioners, are lifelong students of design. They
inquisitively observe the built environment, continually assessing what works and what does
not work—and mentally filing that information away for future reference. Hertzberger
(1991, p. 5) comments on this informal professional development process:
Everything that is absorbed and registered in your mind adds to the collection of ideas
stored in the memory: a sort of library that you can consult whenever a problem arises.
So—essentially—the more you have seen, experienced, and absorbed, the more
points of reference you will have to help you decide which direction to take: your
frame of reference expands.
8.4 	CONCEPTUAL DESIGN PROCESS
The decisions concerning what goes where on the site (i.e., conceptual design) occur in
incremental steps. The conceptual design phase is the time to explore, evaluate, and compare
alternative spatial configurations. Adaptation and revision are inherent in fitting the devel-
opment program to the site. The concept diagram spatially arranges, on the site, the pro-
gram’s major components. The design process then moves to the more detailed design
decisions concerning material selection and surface articulation (Figure 8-2). This approach
to site planning is efficient, effective, and repeatable.
To skip the conceptual design phase is to omit a crucial step in the site-planning process.
Exploring alternative concepts certainly requires time. But this investment can pay sub-
stantial dividends by improving the quality of the final site plan, which—ultimately—
impacts the character, livability, and sustainability of the built environment.
There are two important reasons to design conceptually before advancing to more
detailed site design. First, conceptual design can be done more quickly than detailed design.
Once the program and the site are well understood, multiple concepts can be—and should
be—developed before proceeding to more detailed design (Figure 8-3). Second, conceptual
design is effective, even at small scales of representation (i.e., drawing). The proverbial “back
of the envelope” concept diagram is possible because conceptual design is concerned with
the big picture. A strong concept, or organizing framework, promotes way finding and
creates a strong sense of place.
The conceptual design process can be an exhilarating period of creativity when the
designer develops an organizing structure for the program that responds to the site’s natural
and cultural features and provides the spatial framework for subsequent site and building
design. The process described below is a synthesis of approaches that build on the land
Conceptual Site Design 	251

-- 269 of 387 --

planning process advocated by Ian McHarg (1969) in his classic work, Design with Nature.
This context-sensitive approach is particularly appropriate for large sites with significant
natural or cultural resources.
Step 1: Delineate primary and secondary conservation areas.
Development projects on large sites are more likely to devote a substantial proportion of
the site to open space. This is especially true if the site includes significant environmental
features such as wooded areas, steep slopes, or a stream or wetland. Arendt (1999,
pp. 75–77), in Growing Greener: Putting Conservation into Local Plans and Ordinances, proposes
three principles for designing a site’s conservation open space system:
Conserve the property’s most sensitive natural areas.
Minimize fragmentation of natural areas into smaller, noncontiguous parcels.
Link conservation areas on a property to community and regional open space systems.
Figure 8-2 	Concept initiation (P = paved space; G = green space), development, and refinement for a campus plaza and
promenade at a university in the Midwest. Source: JJR SmithGroup. 	SmithGroupJJR.
252 	Site Analysis

-- 270 of 387 --

In contrast, site-planning projects on smaller urban sites may have little or no conser-
vation open space. Redevelopment projects, especially, tend to be higher density and more
site-intensive. Nevertheless, these sites can still be designed to include outdoor space for
recreation, stormwater management, and other green infrastructure functions.
Step 2: Delineate the areas that are most suitable for development and, within these
areas, delineate individual development “pods” or “envelopes.”
These areas are where the project’s program elements (i.e., buildings and associated
infrastructure, as well as developed open space) will be located.
Attention must be given to the sizes and shapes of the developable pods. Each pod
accommodates not only the anticipated building footprint but also the spaces required for
pedestrian entrances and amenities, vehicle parking and building services, and associated
plantings.
Figure 8-2 	Continued.
Conceptual Site Design 	253

-- 271 of 387 --

Step 3: Locate the primary and secondary circulation networks.
The circulation networks and associated open spaces functionally
and visually link development pods and buildings.
Step 4: Supplement the diagrammatic concept plan with more
information on the desired design character.
The concept plan shows how the project’s major program elements
will be spatially organized on the site. The concept plan can show
functional as well as visual relationships among the program elements.
Existing site features may be included to show how these contextual
elements—or design influences—will be integrated into the develop-
ment or redevelopment (Figure 8-4).
Concept plans are spatially explicit but highly diagrammatic.
Effective diagramming excludes extraneous information (Monmonier,
1996). A city subway map, for example, is useful because of what the
map shows as well as what the map does not show. Buildings, high-
ways, and myriad other types of geographic information are not
included on a subway map for a simple reason: it would reduce the
map’s clarity and, therefore, its effectiveness as a guide to subway
riders.
Conceptual site plans—also known as “bubble diagrams” or
“functional diagrams”—typically show three major site elements: open
spaces (both conservation areas and developed open spaces), circulation
networks, and building pods. The dimensions of streets, buildings, or
other site elements—as well as the types of materials needed to build
these structures—are not typically conveyed on the conceptual site
plan. These design decisions occur during the subsequent design
development and construction documentation phases. The purpose of
the concept plan is to convey the program’s spatial organization
without moving too deeply into detailed design.
The scale of the concept plan determines the amount of detail
shown, and as the site becomes smaller, more information can be
legibly conveyed. The concept plan’s scale is also usually a function of the site’s size. A
concept plan for a 2000-acre (809-hectare) site, for example, might show only general land
use categories, such as residential, commercial, and recreational uses. Within the residential
category, the plan also might identify designated areas for different housing types and
densities. The concept plan for a site of this size might be drawn at a scale of 1:4800
(1 inch 	400 feet or 1 centimeter 	48 meters) or smaller.
In contrast, a concept plan for a 10-acre (4-hectare) site can provide more detailed
information about the proposed development. The plan may, for example, show the
locations of future buildings and building entrances, associated outdoor plazas and sitting
areas, and primary pedestrian and vehicle circulation patterns. This concept plan could be
drawn at a scale of 1:480 (1 inch 	40 feet or 1 centimeter 	4.8 meters).
Concept plans convey information with text and, typically, three graphic elements:
polygons, lines, and points (Table 8-1). These graphic symbols convey information about
the intended organization of the site. Notes or annotations are also useful in conveying

8.4.1 	Polygons
Major site uses are typically portrayed diagrammatically as zones or “bubbles” on a concept
plan. As the concept is refined, these areas may be further subdivided to show building
locations and minor circulation patterns. Concept plans may also show significant natural
areas. As the site-planning process moves further into design development, each area is
designed in more detail (Figure 8-5).
8.4.2 	Lines
Concept plans can diagrammatically show pathways, edges, and desired visual relationships.
Desirable views between locations on and off the site, for example, can be depicted
graphically with arrows and/or annotated labels (Figure 8-6). Showing the locations of
significant natural or cultural features can help explain certain design decisions, such as where

Color, texture, and line weight can help to distinguish these different types of information.

Concept plans can also identify significant nodes and landmarks both on-site and off-site.
These could be locations of proposed buildings, for example, or locations of existing
buildings that will remain. Nodes include:
Building entrances
Plazas and squares
Scenic overlooks
Landmarks visually contrast with their surroundings. Landmarks include:
Historic buildings
Bridges, towers, and other large structures
Mountains, hills, or other topographic features
Mature, specimen trees
8.4.4 	Other Graphics
Conceptual design typically focuses on the plan view or horizontal organization of the site.
Concept plans are often supplemented, however, with other graphics. Especially in urban
settings, the vertical organization of the project’s proposed uses can be expressed concep-
tually. Conceptual sections or three-dimensional aerial perspectives can explain the intended
arrangement of uses within buildings and in adjoining areas outside of the buildings
(Figure 8-7).
Character sketches, or image boards with photographs of relevant precedents, can help to
convey the design intent—before proceeding with detailed design (Figure 8-8). A project’s
character results, in part, from building massing and facade articulation and from
